### [Basis-update and Galerkin time integration in canonical matrix-product-state form](http://arxiv.org/abs/2608.16994v1)

**Authors:** Maximilian Fröhlich, Richard M. Milbradt, Martin Eigel, Aaron Sander, Robert Wille, Christian B. Mendl  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** numerical methods · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2608.16994v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks
**Topic relevance:** `analog quantum simulation` **3/5** · `methods for driven-dissipative` **3/5**

<details open><summary>📷 Fig 1</summary>

<img src="2608.16994_figures/2608.16994_fig1.jpg" width="500"><br>
<sub>FIG. 1. Matrix product state representations. These diagrams define the tensor conventions used in the canoni- cal BUG construction. (a) An MPS represents the state as a chain of site tensors. (b) Left- and right-isometric con- tractions define canonical form and identify the orthogonality center. (c) An MPO represents the Hamiltonian in the same chain geometry. The canonical isometries make the block em- beddings orthonormal and allow the environments and basis updates below to be written as local contractions.</sub>

</details>

<details><summary>📷 Fig 2</summary>

<img src="2608.16994_figures/2608.16994_fig2.jpg" width="500"><br>
<sub>FIG. 2. MPO contractions and environments. How is the global Hamiltonian reduced to the local contractions used during a canonical sweep? (a) The full network gives the Hamiltonian expectation value ⟨ψ| ˆH |ψ⟩. (b) and (c) collect all tensors to the left and right of site i into the environments L[i] and R[i]. Contracting these environments with the local MPO tensor produces the effective Hamiltonian used in the projected update.</sub>

</details>

<details><summary>📷 Fig 3</summary>

<img src="2608.16994_figures/2608.16994_fig3.jpg" width="500"><br>
<sub>FIG. 3. One uncompressed canonical MPS-BUG sweep. How is the BUG basis update realized as a sweep toward a fixed endpoint? (a) A preparation pass records center tensors and environments. (b) These environments define each local effective Hamiltonian. (c) The retained input and local predictor enlarge the next block basis. (d) The overlap between the previous and updated block bases is evaluated recursively. (e) This overlap projects the recorded coefficients into the updated basis and moves the working tensor toward the root. A final projected solve updates the root tensor. Thick bonds denote enlarged virtual dimensions. Compression and normalization are not part of this map.</sub>

</details>

<details><summary>📷 Fig 4</summary>

<img src="2608.16994_figures/2608.16994_fig4.jpg" width="500"><br>
<sub>FIG. 4. Runtime-accuracy trade-off at L = 16. Infidelity versus median runtime for (a) the transverse-field Ising and (b) Haldane-Shastry models, with rmin = 2 and χmax = 512. Marker shape encodes h (circle: 0.01; square: 0.005; triangle: 0.0025), and marker size increases as ϵ tightens from 10−8 to 10−14. Solid blue (BUG) and dashed orange (2-TDVP) lines connect filled Pareto points; open unconnected markers are dominated configurations. Each runtime is the median of three repetitions. Lines guide the eye and do not imply universal dominance.</sub>

</details>

**Summary.** This paper introduces the Basis-Update and Galerkin (BUG) method for time-evolving quantum states represented by Matrix Product States (MPS). It rigorously details the sweep procedure and proves convergence bounds for the scheme. The comparison against 2-TDVP demonstrates that while BUG is often faster, the optimal choice of time integration method is highly dependent on the specific quantum model being simulated.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides advanced numerical tools for simulating quantum dynamics on quantum hardware simulators, directly impacting how researchers can model complex, entangled quantum systems beyond simple Trotter-Suzuki decompositions.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper develops and analyzes a Basis-Update and Galerkin (BUG) time integration scheme for simulating quantum many-body dynamics using Matrix Product States (MPS). It aims to efficiently handle the growth of entanglement during time evolution.

**Main result.** The BUG method is shown to be computationally efficient, often requiring fewer local exponential actions and having lower runtime than 2-TDVP, although the comparison reveals model-dependent trade-offs rather than a universal advantage.

**Method.** The core method involves formulating the time evolution as a sequence of canonical MPS sweeps, which includes a preparation pass, a reverse pass for basis enlargement and coefficient projection, and a final root Galerkin solve.

**Model / system.** The dynamics are studied for quantum many-body systems governed by a time-independent Hermitian Hamiltonian ($\hat{H}$), which is represented as a Matrix Product Operator (MPO). Comparisons are made using the Transverse Field Ising Model (TFIM) and the Haldane-Shastry model.

**Key observables.** Runtime, accuracy (infidelity), and the derived first-order error bound ($\lVert Y_n - A(t_n) Vert \le C_0 e_0 + C_1 \eta + C_2 h$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Timestep ($h$), initial error ($\epsilon_0$), and truncation error ($\eta$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on establishing specific rank conditions for basis retention and assumes that convergence requires both initial error and truncation error to vanish.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the canonical MPS structure, the local effective Hamiltonian environments used in the BUG sweep, and the step-by-step process of the uncompressed canonical MPS-BUG sweep.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the mathematical framework for the BUG sweep (Preparation Pass $	o$ Reverse Pass $	o$ Root Solve). It then derives rigorous error bounds for the uncompressed schedule. Finally, it compares the method's performance against 2-TDVP on specific physical models.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Matrix product state algorithms must enlarge their bond spaces as entanglement grows and compress them to control cost. We formulate basis-update and Galerkin (BUG) time integration as a sequence of canonical MPS sweeps for Hamiltonians represented as matrix product operators. We show when two natural basis updates produce the same trial space and when transporting coefficients between successive bases preserves the represented state. Under these conditions, the existing first-order error bound for uncompressed tree-tensor-network BUG also applies to the alternating-endpoint MPS schedule. We verify the uncompressed implementation against an independent six-site calculation. We then compare BUG with two-site TDVP for 16-site transverse-field Ising and Haldane-Shastry dynamics. At matched timestep and truncation settings, BUG performs fewer local exponential actions and has lower runtime. These settings do not produce equal accuracy. The runtime versus accuracy curves cross for the Ising model and are close for the Haldane-Shastry model. The comparison therefore identifies model-dependent trade-offs rather than a general advantage for either method.

</details>

**Summary.** This work analyzes residual noise in neutral-atom quantum computers, showing that simply meeting minimum physical spacing is insufficient for reliable computation. The authors quantify structured residual coupling, demonstrating that this noise can survive Quantum Error Correction if the spacing is too small. They advocate for new compilers that jointly optimize physical geometry, error absorption, and scheduling time.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a concrete, physics-grounded metric (residual exposure) that must be incorporated into quantum compiler design, moving beyond simple geometric feasibility checks.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the gap between the minimum geometric spacing required for physical gate operation and the actual safety margin against residual noise in neutral-atom quantum processors.

**Main result.** Residual van der Waals coupling, which is structured and correlated, persists even after Quantum Error Correction (QEC) if the physical spacing is too small, necessitating spacing-aware compiler evaluations.

**Method.** The authors combine fixed-schedule residual replay, surface-code simulation with matched correlated decoding, and fresh recompilation analysis to quantify residual exposure and its impact on logical reliability and makespan.

**Model / system.** The system is a neutral-atom processor using Rydberg-mediated entangling gates on spatially arranged qubit arrays. The residual interaction is modeled by the van der Waals tail, leading to a correlated ZZ-like fault.

**Key observables.** Residual exposure ($E_S$, $A_S$), logical error rate after QEC, and compilation makespan cost.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Inter-gate spacing (e.g., 10 $\mu$m vs. 15 $\mu$m), two-qubit gate-error budget ($p_{2q}$), and code distance.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The residual crosstalk is treated as a structured correlated fault source tied to simultaneous gate geometry, and the analysis relies on specific decoding techniques (BP+OSD).

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the residual coupling dependence on spacing, show the structured, localized nature of residual exposure at the physical layer, and compare the logical response to these faults across different spacing regimes.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first establishing the physical model of residual coupling, then simulating its effect on fixed schedules, followed by analyzing its impact on logical reliability via QEC, and concluding with a discussion of the complex trade-off between residual suppression and scheduling overhead.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Neutral-atom processors rely on spatially arranged qubit arrays and parallel Rydberg entangling gates for scalable execution. Their compilers enforce geometric spacing rules for simultaneous gates, yet legal separation does not make residual van der Waals coupling disappear. This paper studies that gap between geometric legality and residual noise by treating entangling-zone spacing as a cross-layer reliability-parallelism variable anchored to experimental neutral-atom geometry. We combine fixed-schedule residual replay, surface-code simulation with matched correlated decoding, and fresh recompilation to connect spacing to physical residual exposure, logical reliability, and makespan cost. The results show that near-floor spacing can produce structured correlated exposure that is visible both at the physical layer and, in the tightest case, after quantum error correction (QEC). Modest geometric slack strongly suppresses this residual contribution, but the timing cost of looser spacing is mediated by placement and scheduling rather than by a simple monotonic slowdown. These findings distinguish hardware legality from residual-noise safety and motivate spacing-aware compiler evaluations that report physical geometry, QEC absorption, and scheduling cost together.

</details>

**Summary.** This work demonstrates that the nonequilibrium heat capacity can act as a sensitive thermodynamic probe for structural phase transitions in active matter. By modeling single and interacting active particles, the authors show that sharp peaks in the heat capacity signal the onset or reorganization of jammed or shape-transitional states. This suggests a powerful experimental avenue for characterizing non-equilibrium phase transitions.

**Why it may be interesting.** The connection drawn between non-equilibrium thermodynamics (heat capacity) and non-equilibrium phase transitions (active matter) provides a novel, measurable diagnostic tool applicable to complex, non-equilibrium many-body systems.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates whether nonequilibrium heat capacity can serve as a measurable thermodynamic signature for structural phase transitions, such as shape or jamming, occurring in active matter systems driven far from equilibrium.

**Main result.** The nonequilibrium heat capacity exhibits distinct, non-monotonic peaks corresponding to the structural transitions, confirming its potential as a calorimetric probe for these active matter phase changes.

**Method.** The authors calculate the excess heat and nonequilibrium heat capacity by analyzing the relaxation dynamics of the system's probability distribution following a small temperature perturbation.

**Model / system.** The models include single active particles confined between boundaries and interacting run-and-tumble (RTP) particles on a periodic lattice, whose dynamics are governed by Markov jump processes.

**Key observables.** Nonequilibrium heat capacity ($C$), stationary density profiles ($ho_i$), and mean block size ($\ell$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Persistence rate ($\alpha$), system size ($L$), and temperature perturbation ($\Delta T$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on calculating the *excess* heat and assumes local detailed balance for the spatial hopping rates in the lattice model.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the structural crossover (e.g., mean block size vs. persistence rate) alongside the corresponding non-monotonic heat capacity response for both single and interacting particle systems.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the theoretical framework for calculating nonequilibrium heat capacity. It then applies this to the single-particle case (boundary effects) and extends the analysis to interacting particles (jamming transitions), concluding with numerical methods for AC calorimetry.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Persistence influences the stationary states of active particles, producing boundary accumulation at the single-particle level, and clustering or jamming in interacting systems. These features disappear as the persistence decreases and the system approaches a more passive-like stationary state. We show that these transitions have a distinct calorimetric signature. Using a lattice run-and-tumble dynamics, consistent with local detailed balance, we compute the nonequilibrium heat capacity from the excess heat released following a small temperature perturbation. For a single particle confined between reflecting boundaries, the heat capacity develops a maximum in the persistence regime corresponding to shape transition. Adding an exclusion interaction to the active particles on a periodic lattice, the reorganization of jammed clusters produces a corresponding peak in the thermal response. We also discuss the impact of the time-symmetric part of the transition rates, and show the possibility of seeing the same signatures of heat response in experiments by AC calorimetry. Our results show that nonequilibrium heat capacities can serve as calorimetric probes of nonequilibrium phase transitions.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper introduces Multi-shift Quantum Imaginary-Time Evolution (MS-QITE) to overcome limitations in simulating quantum many-body systems on quantum computers. By engineering interference using a distribution of energy shifts, MS-QITE improves the efficiency and stability of ground-state and thermal-state preparation. This technique offers a general framework for optimizing quantum algorithms by exploiting spectral transformations.

**Why it may be interesting.** The technique provides a powerful, interference-based resource to optimize quantum algorithms for simulating thermal and ground states, which is a central theme in quantum simulation.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the challenge of efficiently implementing imaginary-time evolution (ITE) for ground-state and thermal-state preparation on quantum computers.

**Main result.** The proposed Multi-shift Quantum Imaginary-Time Evolution (MS-QITE) uses a distribution of energy shifts to engineer interference, significantly improving the stability and reducing the required resources for state preparation.

**Method.** The core method involves modifying the standard ITE by introducing a distribution of energy shifts, which transforms the evolution into a controllable real-time superposition integral.

**Model / system.** The method is tested on quantum many-body systems, specifically using the transverse-field Ising model Hamiltonian. The implementation is demonstrated in both Monte Carlo sampling and continuous-variable-assisted schemes.

**Key observables.** Ground-state energy error, success probability, and trace distance for thermal state preparation.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The energy shift distribution $f(q)$, the number of shifts $k$, and the imaginary time $	au$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The approach relies on the fact that energy shifts generate relative phases that interfere when implemented on a quantum computer, even if they are trivial in normalization.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare probability densities with and without multi-shift, show ground-state energy error vs. cutoff time, and illustrate success probability/resource consumption for thermal state preparation.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces MS-QITE by relating it to the real-time superposition of evolutions. It then details its application in Monte Carlo and continuous-variable settings, culminating in numerical results on the Ising model.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Energy shifting is usually trivial in imaginary-time evolution because it changes only the normalization of the evolved state. On a quantum computer, however, imaginary-time evolution can be implemented as a coherent or sampled superposition of real-time evolutions, in which energy shifts generate relative phases that can interfere. Here we introduce multi-shift quantum imaginary-time evolution (MS-QITE), which uses a distribution of energy shifts to engineer interference and optimize different implementations. In a Monte Carlo realization, multi-shift reshapes the sampling distribution and concentrates it within a shorter real-time window, thereby reducing the required Hamiltonian-evolution time and improving the stability of ground-state-energy estimation. In a continuous-variable-assisted realization, it permits postselection over a finite quadrature interval rather than near a single quadrature value, substantially reducing resource consumption while retaining accurate thermal-state preparation. Numerical results for transverse-field Ising models demonstrate that energy shifts provide a useful interference-based approach for optimizing quantum imaginary-time evolution.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper proposes 'attoquants,' a new quantum state concept, to explain attosecond pulse generation in High-Harmonic Generation (HHG) without relying on the classical requirement of phase-locking. By analyzing the coherent permanent state, the authors prove that this entangled state inherently supports pulse trains. This offers a quantum-mechanical framework for understanding ultrafast light sources driven by collective atomic effects.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly bridges quantum optics and attosecond physics by proposing a fundamentally quantum mechanism (entanglement) to achieve pulse synthesis, moving beyond semi-classical approximations.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To develop a quantum optical concept for attosecond pulse generation that bypasses the classical requirement of mutual phase-locking among emitted harmonics.

**Main result.** The authors introduce 'attoquants,' a novel class of multimode quantum states, demonstrating that their electric-field expectation value yields an attosecond pulse train without requiring mode-locking.

**Method.** The analysis involves constructing and characterizing the coherent permanent state (an attoquant) and calculating quantum diagnostics like photon statistics and logarithmic negativity.

**Model / system.** The system is High-Harmonic Generation (HHG), modeled quantum mechanically as a harmonic radiation field composed of quantized electromagnetic modes. The core theoretical tool is the attoquant state, which is a permutation-symmetric superposition of coherent states.

**Key observables.** Electric-field expectation value, photon statistics, Wigner function, and logarithmic negativity.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The structure of the harmonic field (discrete frequencies $(2q+1)\omega$) and the concept of intermodal entanglement.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis is theoretical, offering a phenomenological interpretation for the physical origin of these entangled states based on multi-atom effects during HHG.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the electric-field distribution for different pulse synthesis methods (mean-field vs. field-variance) and compare the temporal structure of field intensity for mode-locked versus random phase states.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from establishing the classical limitations of HHG to introducing the quantum attoquant concept, analytically deriving its properties, and finally providing a physical interpretation rooted in collective multi-atom radiation.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

High-harmonic generation (HHG) is conventionally understood to produce attosecond pulse trains only when the emitted harmonics are mutually phase-locked, in accordance with the classical theory of mode-locking. Such locking is necessary for pulse synthesis when the harmonic field is in a separable multimode quantum state. This requirement, however, can be circumvented entirely for harmonic combs exhibiting intermodal entanglement. We introduce a novel family of multimode quantum states, termed attoquants: states associated with highly structured pulse trains that are inherently insensitive to relative phases. As a concrete example, we analyse the coherent permanent state, constructed as a completely symmetric superposition of products of coherent states over all permutations of a fixed parameter set. It is shown analytically that its electric-field expectation value is a locked Fourier superposition of the driving-field harmonics, yielding an attosecond pulse train without requiring mode-locking. We further calculate the photon statistics, the Wigner function, and the logarithmic negativity of this state, confirming it to be genuinely nonclassical and entangled. Finally, we offer a phenomenological interpretation for the generation of such entangled states during HHG, as a consequence of multi-atom effects. Since the coherence volume of the driving field greatly exceeds that of its harmonics, a collectively-driven cluster of atoms can radiate the individual harmonics as spatially resolved, but fundamentally indistinguishable sources. This can generate the permutation-symmetric entanglement structure of an attoquant.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper presents a resource-efficient quantum circuit implementation for simulating the magnetic part of the Hamiltonian in non-Abelian lattice gauge theories. By developing a block encoding oracle for the plaquette operator, the authors significantly reduce the computational overhead associated with simulating these complex quantum field theories. This work advances the feasibility of simulating QCD dynamics on fault-tolerant quantum hardware.

**Why it may be interesting.** The techniques developed—block encoding, programmed rotations, and lookup-based oracle construction—are highly relevant for implementing complex Hamiltonian terms in quantum simulation algorithms.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Developing efficient quantum simulation methods for lattice gauge theories, particularly for real-time dynamics, which are challenging due to complex, correlated transitions in the magnetic part of the Hamiltonian.

**Main result.** The authors propose an efficient block encoding oracle for the plaquette operator in the irrep basis, significantly reducing the resource cost compared to previous methods.

**Method.** The method uses a block encoding oracle implemented via programmed rotation trees fed by quantum lookups of classically pre-computed, factorized matrix elements.

**Model / system.** The system is non-Abelian lattice gauge theory, exemplified by Lattice QCD (SU(3)). The Hamiltonian involves electric and magnetic terms, with the magnetic part built from the plaquette operator.

**Key observables.** The simulation targets the evolution governed by the full Hamiltonian, with specific focus on the plaquette operator $\square_p$ and the electric energy term $E_R$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The resource cost is estimated in T gates (e.g., $10^5$ to $10^6$ for SU(3)), and the logical qubit count scales with the lattice volume $L^3$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The simulation is designed for the fault-tolerant, error-corrected quantum computation regime, and the method relies on truncating the Hilbert space using a Casimir cutoff $B$.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the components of a plaquette and its state, while Figure 16 details the quantum circuit structure for calculating the electric energy term $H_E$.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by reviewing the Hamiltonian and basis, detailing the decomposition into electric and magnetic parts, describing the magnetic block encoding, presenting resource estimates, and discussing its application in QSVT.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Gauge theories like lattice QCD present a complex problem for quantum simulation. In a basis where the electric part of the Hamiltonian is simple, the magnetic part, generally expressed as a sum over the plaquette operators of the lattice, is quite complicated, producing correlated transitions between several link and site degrees of freedom. We provide an efficient block encoding of the plaquette operator in the irrep basis, a refinement of the electric basis where the internal gauge-variant degrees of freedom are integrated out. The construction removes the plaquette matrix element scaling wall which has been a significant barrier for other approaches in this basis. The algorithm leverages a convenient factorization property of the matrix elements, cheap classical precomputation, and quantum oracles built from lookup tables and programmed rotations.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper proposes a method to generate rotational chirality in achiral molecules by manipulating their angular momentum using precisely timed microwave or THz/optical pulse sequences. By satisfying strict symmetry breaking conditions, the induced chirality can be measured using Photoelectron Circular Dichroism. This opens a new avenue for studying time-odd chiroptical phenomena in fundamental molecular systems.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work is highly relevant to AMO physics as it tackles the fundamental control of molecular quantum states using tailored electromagnetic pulses, pushing the boundaries of chiroptical spectroscopy in the gas phase.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the challenge of generating and investigating time-odd (false) chirality in isolated, achiral molecules by controlling their rotational dynamics.

**Main result.** The authors demonstrate that rotational chirality can be induced in randomly oriented molecules using specific sequences of microwave or combined THz/optical pulses, which can be probed via Photoelectron Circular Dichroism (PECD).

**Method.** The theoretical framework involves deriving symmetry conditions for creating chiral superpositions of rotational states, while the experimental proposals utilize pulsed laser/microwave excitation sequences.

**Model / system.** The system studied is the rigid asymmetric rotor (e.g., $	ext{CH}_2	ext{O}$, $	ext{COFCl}$), whose dynamics are governed by the time-dependent Schrödinger equation under external pulsed fields.

**Key observables.** The expectation value of the angular momentum projection ($\langle \hat{J}_z angle$) and the Photoelectron Circular Dichroism (PECD) signal are the key diagnostics for induced chirality.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The required excitation involves at least three microwave or polarization directions (X, Y, Z) to break both spatial and molecular point group symmetries.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes the rigid rotor approximation and that the probe pulse is short compared to the rotational dynamics timescale.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the excitation schemes (microwave/THz/optical) and show the time evolution of the induced angular momentum ($\langle \hat{J}_a angle$) and the corresponding PECD signal, demonstrating controllable handedness.

**Paper structure.** The paper moves from defining the problem (time-odd chirality) to deriving the necessary symmetry conditions for state superposition, detailing specific pulse sequences for microwave and optical excitation, and finally presenting PECD as the measurable diagnostic.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Achiral molecules can be prepared in superposition states that are chiral. Here, we propose angular momentum orientation in the molecular frame to achieve the required symmetry breaking, exerting rotational control without the need for laboratory frame orientation. We derive the conditions for chiral rotational dynamics from the requirement to simultaneously break the continuous spatial rotational symmetry and the molecular point group symmetry. This can be achieved by three microwave pulses as well as two non-resonant optical pulses in combination with a THz pulse or three THz pulses, all with mutually orthogonal polarization directions, and the ensuing dynamics can be probed by photoelectron circular dichroism. Our results open the way for distinguishing structural from dynamical enantioselectivity and investigating time-odd chiroptical phenomena in randomly oriented molecules.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper reviews the use of NV-DEER spectroscopy to characterize paramagnetic defects in CVD diamond. By tracking defect formation and transformation through annealing and irradiation, the authors developed a model linking defect control to the achievable spin coherence time. This establishes the method as a powerful tool for optimizing diamond for advanced quantum sensing applications.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a detailed experimental roadmap for controlling solid-state spin baths, which is crucial for developing robust quantum memories and sensors based on solid-state qubits.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To understand the fabrication process and optimize the material quality of CVD diamond for use in quantum sensing by characterizing the evolution of paramagnetic defects.

**Main result.** The study successfully mapped the transformation of various defects (X-centers, vacancies, hydrogen species) during annealing, demonstrating that NV-DEER spectroscopy is a powerful tool for achieving high spin coherence times.

**Method.** The research uses Double Electron-Electron Resonance (DEER) spectroscopy combined with controlled thermal annealing and electron irradiation of CVD diamond samples.

**Model / system.** The physical system is CVD diamond containing NV centers and various paramagnetic impurities (P1, vacancies, hydrogen). A Hamiltonian-based model is used to interpret the dipolar coupling measured by DEER.

**Key observables.** Spin coherence time ($T_2$), DEER spectral features corresponding to specific defect species ($	ext{V}^-$, $	ext{NVH}^-$), and defect concentrations.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Annealing temperatures ($650^\circ	ext{C}$ to $1200^\circ	ext{C}$), electron irradiation dose, and the coupling strengths between different spin bath components.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on developing and applying a comprehensive spin bath model that accounts for independent couplings between multiple defect types.

**Figures summary.** The notes mention Figure 1 showing the growth design and nitrogen incorporation trends for multilayer diamond samples.

**Paper structure.** The paper follows a structure of reviewing the state-of-the-art, detailing the experimental methodology (sample preparation, irradiation, annealing), presenting the defect evolution tracking via DEER, and concluding with a model linking defect control to high coherence times.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

The aim of this paper is to provide the reader with a review and current state of the art of the fabrication of high T2 coherence diamond, optimised by the use of double electron-electron resonance (DEER) spectroscopy. Using DEER, we study the formation, transformation, and annealing of paramagnetic defects in as-grown CVD diamond and after post-processing. Electron irradiation leads to the formation of an additional S = 1/2 resonance in the DEER spectrum, which we consider to be a composite X ensemble. By tracking the concentrations of X and P1 point defects during annealing from 650C to 1200C, we find that the X ensemble initially consists of a mixture of V- spins and interstitial spins, which disappear at about 650C. Vacancies migrate during annealing, forming clusters that persist to 1000C and disappear upon annealing at 1200C, contributing to the X ensemble signal. We have developed a model of the influence of the mixed spin bath on the coherence of NV centers, which includes independent couplings with P1 centers, V-, divacancies, and interstitials. Detailed DEER studies allowed us to reveal and resolve a weak signal from two additional S = 1/2 species associated with hydrogen: NVH- and consistent with a substitutional hydrogen defect, which overlaps the vacancy spectral line. Taken together, these results show that the NV-DEER method is a powerful tool for investigating paramagnetic defects in diamond with high precision and nanoscale resolution, essential for material optimisation. The achieved high T2 coherence time is consistent with the spin bath model, and the crystals reach the quality required for advanced quantum sensing applications.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper develops a stable training protocol for using Recurrent Neural Networks to represent quantum many-body wave functions. It overcomes the known instability of second-order optimizers like minSR by introducing regularization techniques. The stabilized method proves highly effective, offering a powerful tool for simulating complex quantum Hamiltonians.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly tackles the practical limitations of using advanced quantum machine learning techniques (RNN-NQS) for simulating complex quantum matter, providing a robust optimization pathway for variational quantum simulation.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the instability of using second-order optimization methods, like minimum-step stochastic reconfiguration (minSR), when training Recurrent Neural Network Quantum States (RNN-NQS).

**Main result.** By applying simple regularization techniques to the minSR update, the authors stabilize the training process, showing that the method outperforms the Adam optimizer on several benchmark quantum models.

**Method.** The core method involves variational optimization of the energy expectation value using RNNs to parameterize the wave function, coupled with advanced analysis of the Fisher information matrix spectrum to stabilize the natural gradient descent update.

**Model / system.** The framework is applied to quantum many-body systems, including the 1D transverse-field Ising model, 1D cluster state, 2D Heisenberg, and J1-J2 models, using RNNs to represent the wave function $|\Psi_	hetaangle$.

**Key observables.** Energy expectation value $\langle\hat{H}angle$, the Fisher information matrix spectrum, and the condition number $\kappa_2(S_\lambda)$ are key diagnostics.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The regularization parameter $\lambda$ (Tikhonov parameter) and the number of Monte Carlo samples $N_s$ are crucial hyperparameters.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on the i.i.d. sampling assumption for error bounding and assumes that careful preconditioning can mitigate the ill-conditioned nature of the RNN Fisher spectrum.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the schematic structure of RNN wave functions for 1D and 2D lattices, and Figure 6 specifically shows the training stability of minSR as a function of the regularization parameter $\lambda$ and sample size $N_s$.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from introducing RNN-NQS and the optimization goal, to analyzing the inherent instability of minSR due to the RNN structure, developing the regularization technique, and finally benchmarking the stabilized method against Adam on various quantum Hamiltonians.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Neural Quantum States (NQS) provide a powerful neural network-based variational framework for representing many-body wave functions and solving for ground states. Recurrent Neural Networks (RNNs) are particularly promising owing to their relatively low computational cost and their autoregressive property, which enables perfect sampling. Recently, RNNs have been reported to be unstable under curvature-based optimizers such as the minimum-step stochastic reconfiguration (minSR) method. In this paper, we address this perceived limitation and show that minSR can be stabilized through simple regularization techniques, enabling robust training of RNN-based NQS with only a few samples. Our approach outperforms the Adam optimizer on the one-dimensional transverse-field Ising model and the one-dimensional cluster state, and provides competitive results on the two-dimensional Heisenberg and $J_1-J_2$ models. This work offers a promising pathway for using modern optimization techniques with autoregressive NQS to address open questions in quantum simulation.

</details>

**Summary.** Researchers used RA-SHG spectroscopy on the CoSi surface to study a CDW transition at 90 K. They found the transition follows the 3D XY critical scaling, which is unexpected for a 2D surface. This suggests that the CDW order parameter has an intrinsic 3D character due to coupling with the underlying bulk topology of the Weyl semimetal.

**Why it may be interesting.** The finding of 3D XY critical behavior on a 2D surface challenges simple dimensionality arguments in critical phenomena, suggesting that topological coupling can induce higher-dimensional phase coherence.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To determine the nature and dimensionality of a Charge Density Wave (CDW) instability occurring on the surface of a Weyl Semimetal.

**Main result.** The CDW transition exhibits critical behavior consistent with the 3D XY universality class, despite being confined to a nominally two-dimensional surface layer.

**Method.** Rotational Anisotropy Second Harmonic Generation (RA-SHG) spectroscopy was used to measure the temperature dependence of the surface nonlinear susceptibility tensor elements.

**Model / system.** The study investigates the CoSi (001) surface, a Weyl Semimetal, where the CDW order parameter is probed. The analysis compares the observed critical exponent to theoretical predictions for phase transitions.

**Key observables.** Second Harmonic Generation (SHG) intensity, which tracks the CDW order parameter amplitude; the critical exponent $\beta$; and the transition temperature $T_{CDW}$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Critical exponent $\beta = 0.30 \pm 0.03$; Transition temperature $T_{CDW} = 90.0 \pm 0.8$ K; Comparison to 3D XY universality class.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The apparent contradiction between 2D surface confinement and 3D XY scaling is resolved by assuming the CDW order parameter possesses an intrinsic three-dimensional structure due to coupling between surface sublayers.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the raw RA-SHG data showing anisotropy changes across the transition, and plots detail the temperature dependence of background-subtracted SHG intensity and ratios of tensor elements.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the experimental setup and the CDW transition using RA-SHG. It then analyzes the temperature scaling of the SHG signal to extract the critical exponent, concluding that the 3D XY class applies due to underlying 3D coupling mechanisms.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Using temperature-dependent rotational anisotropy second harmonic generation (RA-SHG), we identify a charge density wave (CDW) instability on the CoSi (001) face with an onset temperature at $90.0 \pm 0.8$ K. The SHG response tracks the order parameter amplitude, dominated by two nonlinear tensor elements, whose background-subtracted intensity evolves with temperature as a power law. The extracted critical exponent $β= 0.30 \pm 0.03$ is consistent with the 3D XY universality class, an unexpected result for a nominally two-dimensional surface layer. No corresponding anomaly is observed in the bulk response, establishing the transition as a purely surface-driven phase transition. We attribute this unexpected scaling to the coupling between surface Fermi-arc states and the bulk topology they are tied to, grounded in a previously observed intra-unit-cell phase relationship between surface sublayers that gives the order parameter intrinsic three-dimensional structure.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper applies tensor-network methods, specifically Matrix Product States, to study interacting quantum field theories in Anti-de Sitter space. By analyzing the energy spectrum and performing finite-size scaling, the authors non-perturbatively characterize the critical point of $\phi^4$ theory in $	ext{AdS}_2$. This work successfully maps the bulk and boundary physics onto the known 2D Ising universality class.

**Why it may be interesting.** The application of tensor networks (MPS) to study critical phenomena in curved spacetime provides a powerful, non-perturbative tool for analyzing strongly correlated quantum systems, bridging condensed matter critical theory with gravity/QFT.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates interacting quantum field theories in Anti-de Sitter (AdS) space, focusing on determining bulk criticality and extracting the low-lying boundary spectra.

**Main result.** The authors successfully compute boundary scaling dimensions non-perturbatively for $\phi^4$ theory in $	ext{AdS}_2$, locating the bulk $\mathbb{Z}_2$ symmetry-breaking transition consistent with the 2D Ising universality class.

**Method.** The research employs a tensor-network approach based on Matrix Product States (MPS) and utilizes finite-size scaling in the AdS radius ($R$) to probe critical behavior.

**Model / system.** The system is an interacting scalar quantum field theory, specifically $\phi^4$ theory, formulated in global Anti-de Sitter space ($	ext{AdS}_2$). The Hamiltonian is discretized using a lattice formulation.

**Key observables.** Low-lying boundary spectra, bulk energy levels, critical exponents, and central charge.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The AdS radius ($R$), the coupling constant ($\lambda$), and the lattice spacing.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The use of a lattice Hamiltonian formulation allows for independent variation of the lattice spacing and the AdS radius $R$.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the theoretical framework by relating the global AdS spectrum to boundary operators via the bulk-boundary OPE. It then details the lattice discretization of the Hamiltonian and applies the MPS/tensor-network methods to compute spectra and analyze finite-size scaling near critical points.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We study interacting scalar quantum field theories in anti-de Sitter (AdS) space using a tensor-network approach based on matrix product states. We develop methods for extracting low-lying boundary spectra from global-AdS energy levels, probing bulk criticality through finite-size scaling in the AdS radius, and identifying conformal boundary conditions. Applying these methods to scalar $φ^4$ theory in $\mathrm{AdS}_2$, we compute the lowest non-trivial $\mathbb{Z}_2$-odd and even boundary scaling dimensions non-perturbatively. We then locate the bulk $\mathbb{Z}_2$ symmetry-breaking transition and extract critical exponents and central charge consistent with the 2D Ising universality class. At the critical point, we determine the low-lying boundary spectrum and use it to identify the $\mathbb{Z}_2$-preserving free/ordinary Ising conformal boundary condition, thereby characterising both the bulk and boundary universality classes.

</details>

**Type:** theory · **Category:** other · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2608.17984v1>

**Summary.** This paper details the theoretical design of planar Penning traps intended for single-electron qubits. The authors develop advanced analytical and simulation techniques to ensure the trapping potential is highly harmonic, which is crucial for stable quantum operations. They conclude that a specific four-electrode geometry is required to overcome the limitations of smaller, microfabricated traps.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses the engineering challenges of realizing solid-state or microfabricated quantum hardware by providing rigorous theoretical guidelines for optimizing trap potentials for single-electron qubits.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary goal is to design planar Penning traps that maintain a highly harmonic confining potential for single-electron qubits used in quantum information processing.

**Main result.** The authors demonstrate that a four-electrode, three-gap planar trap geometry is necessary and sufficient to achieve the required harmonicity, even when accounting for finite electrode gaps and boundaries.

**Method.** The work combines fully analytic derivations of the electrostatic potential with detailed comparison against finite-element simulations to optimize trap geometry and electrode voltages.

**Model / system.** The system is a planar Penning trap confining a single electron using a combination of a magnetic field and voltages on concentric electrodes. The potential is modeled by superimposing contributions from these electrodes, requiring careful treatment of finite gaps and boundaries.

**Key observables.** Harmonicity of the axial potential, axial oscillation frequency ($\omega_z$), and the thermally induced frequency width ($\sigma_f$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Millimetre size scale and below (sub-mm), requiring consideration of relative gap sizes; the trap must be optimized to ensure $\sigma_f \ll \gamma_z$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes the potential can be modeled by analyzing contributions from individual electrodes, and that the trap can be optimized to minimize anharmonicities despite the planar geometry's inherent limitations compared to 3D traps.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the geometry of the four-electrode planar Penning trap, its cross-section, and the relationship between required electrostatic curvature, trap depth, and desired axial frequency.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by establishing the need for high harmonicity, developing the theoretical framework for planar traps with finite gaps, showing that simple designs fail, and finally presenting the optimization procedure using analytic and simulation methods to achieve stable confinement.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We present a detailed account of the design choices required for planar Penning traps that feature a highly harmonic confining potential. High harmonicity is indispensable for a number of applications, particularly for confinement of single electrons as qubits in quantum-information processing. The present work extends previous studies [D. Goldmann and G. Gabrielse, Phys. Rev. A 81, 052335 (2010)] by a fully analytic treatment of finite electrode gaps and their relevance for small traps on the millimetre size scale and below, when relative gap sizes are non-negligible. We derive the overall trap potential with a particular focus on different models of finite-gap potentials and show how to find the optimum trap geometry and electrode voltages to minimize anharmonicities. The analytic calculations are compared with detailed finite-element simulations.

</details>

**Authors:** Rui-Yan Chen, Zhaoxin Liang, Gao Xianlong  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** quantum gases · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2608.17473v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks
**Topic relevance:** `Keldysh / 2PI / non-Gaussian methods` **3/5** · `analog quantum simulation` **1/5**

Our one- loop and two-loop results are compared with Petrov’s one- loop theory [12]. The curves are labeled in the figure. E is in units of 10−4ℏ2/(2ma5) and n in units of 10−3a−3.</sub>

Other parameters are as in Fig. 1. The curves confirm the scaling identity (61): E(r) = (1+1/r)E(1)/2. E is in units of 10−4ℏ2/(2m1a5) and n in units of 10−3a−3.</sub>

**Summary.** This theoretical paper calculates the universal quantum corrections to two-body correlations in a weakly interacting binary Bose mixture using the CJT effective action formalism. By employing a saddle-point approximation, the authors derive the energy density as a low-density expansion, showing that the mixture's corrections map simply to single-component results. The theory successfully reproduces known equations of state and analyzes the impact of mass imbalance on the system's energy.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a highly rigorous, analytical framework for calculating quantum corrections in multi-component quantum gases, extending established results like the LHY corrections to mixtures and providing insights into mass imbalance effects.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates the universal quantum corrections to two-body correlations in a weakly interacting binary Bose mixture at zero temperature.

**Main result.** The universal quantum corrections map simply to the single-component case, evaluated using an effective scattering length $\delta a_\sigma = a_{\sigma\sigma} - a_{12}$. The theory successfully reproduces known results like Petrov's EOS and yields beyond-Lee-Huang-Yang corrections.

**Method.** The authors employ the Cornwall-Jackiw-Tomboulis (CJT) two-particle-irreducible effective action formalism, utilizing a saddle-point approximation on the interspecies coupling via Hubbard-Stratonovich transformations.

**Model / system.** The system is a zero-temperature binary Bose mixture, modeled by an action involving intra-species ($g_1, g_2$) and interspecies ($g_{12}$) couplings. The analysis is performed in the weak interspecies-coupling regime.

**Key observables.** Ground-state energy density, quantum depletion, and chemical potentials.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Weak interspecies-coupling regime ($|a_{12}| \ll |a_{11}|, |a_{22}|$), low-density expansion, and mass imbalance ratio ($m_1/m_2$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The primary assumption is the validity of the saddle-point approximation for the interspecies field, which restricts the analysis to the weak-coupling limit.

**Figures summary.** Figure 3 illustrates the energy density $E$ dependence on total density $n$ for various mass ratios, confirming the predicted scaling identity $E(r) = (1+1/r)E(1)/2$.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops the theory by first applying Hubbard-Stratonovich transformations and performing a saddle-point treatment. It then calculates the energy density via a two-loop expansion, deriving the single-component mapping and analyzing the scaling behavior due to mass imbalance.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We investigate universal quantum corrections to two-body correlations in a zero-temperature binary Bose mixture using the Cornwall-Jackiw-Tomboulis two-particle-irreducible effective action formalism. In the weak interspecies-coupling regime, a saddle-point treatment based on Hubbard-Stratonovich transformations can be combined with a two-loop expansion and a gapless Hartree-Fock correction, thereby preserving the Goldstone theorem and reducing the coupled two-component problem to two analytically solvable single-component theories. Within this framework, we derive the ground-state energy density as a low-density expansion in the gas parameter, together with the quantum depletion and chemical potentials. The results exhibit a simple mapping to the single-component case: the universal quantum corrections of the mixture are obtained by evaluating the known single-component series at an effective scattering length $a_{σσ}-a_{12}$ for each species, where $a_{σσ}$ and $a_{12}$ are the intra- and interspecies $s$-wave scattering lengths. This reproduces Petrov's equation of state at one-loop order in the weak-coupling limit and yields beyond-Lee-Huang-Yang corrections at two-loop order. We also analyze the role of mass imbalance, which enters the energy density through the exact rescaling factor $(1+m_{1}/m_{2})/2$.

</details>

**Authors:** Won Joon Yun, Andrew Nemec, Jonathan M. Baker  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** quantum information and computing · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2608.17870v1>

On the left, the code carries the logical ¯Z and ¯ X, where dark plaquettes are Z- type checks, light plaquettes are X-type checks, and orange marks triggered Z-type checks. On the right, the two-qubit error X0X3 (probability ∼p2) and the single-qubit error X6 (∼p) trigger exactly the same checks. The decoder picks the likelier X6, and when the true error is X0X3, the residual is the logical ¯ X. No decoder can avoid this failure; deformation can suppress it.</sub>

<details><summary>📷 Fig 4</summary>

<img src="2608.17870_figures/2608.17870_fig4.jpg" width="500"><br>
<sub>Fig. 4. Overview of CHAMELEON, the compile-time pass on one synthetic mixed-bias d=3 map (η=10, p=0.005). It takes the per-qubit noise map and the code as input. Step 1 finds the ambiguity operators of the undeformed code, and only the likeliest X and Z operators are drawn for legibility, a cost paid once and independent of the calibration map. Step 2 minimizes U, an approximation to a bound on the LER, over the binary space (2n). Step 3 extends to the full frame space (6n) with the cross-entropy method, reducing the bound to U6n. The optimized frame F ⋆is then deployed without decoder validation.</sub>

</details>

<details><summary>📷 Fig 5</summary>

<img src="2608.17870_figures/2608.17870_fig5.jpg" width="500"><br>
<sub>Fig. 5. Surrogate U vs. measured worst-axis LER for 35 frames per cell (4 fixed baselines, the deployed CHAMELEON frame, and 30 random full frames), at code capacity with p=0.02 and ≥256 ambiguity events per frame. Columns are ordered by the bias strength of the map (fraction of qubits with ηq&gt;1.5, from left to right 6, 13, 43, 67%). Surrogate fidelity rises with bias. On the least-biased map (Miami) the surface-code frames are a near-flat tie and ranking is noise-dominated, while on BB36 ρ reaches 0.84–0.98 on every map, and CHAMELEON lands in the lowest-U, lowest-LER corner of every panel.</sub>

</details>

**Summary.** This paper introduces Chameleon, a fast compiler designed to optimize Clifford deformations for quantum error correction codes operating under realistic, biased noise. By replacing intractable full simulations with an analytical upper bound on failure probabilities, it efficiently finds optimal noise mitigation strategies. This significantly improves the achievable logical error rates while making the optimization process computationally feasible for real-world hardware constraints.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on superconducting qubits, the development of a fast, general-purpose compiler for optimizing error correction protocols against realistic, non-i.i.d. noise is highly relevant for any field utilizing quantum computation, including AMO platforms.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary goal is to reduce the Logical Error Rate (LER) in fault-tolerant quantum computing when physical qubits suffer from non-uniform, biased noise.

**Main result.** The proposed compiler, Chameleon, achieves significant LER reductions (e.g., 19% for surface codes) while drastically reducing classical computation time from days to minutes.

**Method.** It employs a novel compiler that approximates the LER using an analytical upper bound on ambiguity operators, avoiding computationally prohibitive full simulations.

**Model / system.** The work models superconducting quantum hardware subject to spatially non-uniform Pauli noise (X/Z bias). It applies to general Quantum Error Correction (QEC) codes like the surface code, color code, and BB72 code.

**Key observables.** Logical Error Rate (LER), correlation coefficient ($ho$) between the surrogate score and actual LER, and classical computation time.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Noise bias ($\eta_q$), code distance ($d$), and the specific structure of the error-correcting code used.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The method relies on approximating the true LER using an analytical upper bound derived from the ambiguity operators, assuming this bound strongly preserves the ranking of candidate deformations.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the non-uniform noise bias on a device layout and compare LER reduction achieved by Chameleon against other policies on a sampled map for a surface code.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the problem of biased noise in QEC, details the limitations of existing global/local deformation techniques, proposes the Chameleon compiler using an analytic surrogate objective, and validates its performance across multiple code families and noise models.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

In fault-tolerant quantum computing systems with biased noise, Clifford deformation can substantially reduce the logical error rate (LER) without additional physical hardware overhead, such as extra qubits, syndrome extraction rounds, or code distance. Although Google Willow calibration data shows that $43\%$ of qubits exhibit strong $X/Z$ bias, existing calibration-aware deformation techniques remain impractical: (1) global searches over the $6^n$ deformation choices rely on computing-intensive simulations, and (2) local heuristics often underperform undeformed baselines.   We present Chameleon, a fast, high-performance, and code-agnostic Clifford deformation compiler. We utilize our approximation to tackle a deformation problem based on an analytical bound on the LER. By minimizing this surrogate, Chameleon finds an optimized deformation that empirically reduces the LER with substantially lower computational overhead. In our evaluation, using calibration models derived from real superconducting devices, Chameleon demonstrates that improvements in our surrogate are strongly correlated with actual LER reductions, with an average rank correlation of $ρ=0.8$ and $ρ=0.89$-$0.94$ on the most strongly biased system. It also reduces classical computational time from $1.2$ days to $3.1$ minutes for the BB72 code. Chameleon achieves maximum LER reductions of $19\%$ ($13\%$ on average) for surface codes, $16\%$ ($7\%$) for color codes, and $10\%$ ($4\%$) for bivariate bicycle codes relative to competing baselines. The maximum gains for all code families are observed on the most strongly biased system.

</details>

### [Frustration without Glass in Non-Abelian Simplicial Networks](http://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17817v1)

**Authors:** Xuanhua Wang  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** disordered systems and neural networks · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2608.17817v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks
**Topic relevance:** `analog quantum simulation` **3/5**

(c) The finite-size analysis of G2 adj at representative ∆= 0.6. The approximate N −1 scaling is consistent with Iadj ∼3NCadj remaining a finite value at ∆&gt; ∆c. **Summary.** This work analyzes a non-Abelian gauge network subjected to quenched frustration, modeling structural consistency via plaquette holonomies. By analyzing key correlation measures, the authors demonstrate that the system enters a non-glassy correlated liquid phase rather than freezing into a spin-glass state. This suggests that collective order can persist through geometric dilution of local correlations.

**Why it may be interesting.** The concept of a 'correlated gauge liquid' provides a novel theoretical framework for understanding collective organization in highly frustrated systems, moving beyond standard spin-glass paradigms.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates the collective phase that emerges in systems with non-Abelian holonomies when subjected to quenched, mutually frustrating couplings, aiming to distinguish this from conventional glassy freezing.

**Main result.** Dense frustration leads to a non-glassy correlated gauge liquid, characterized by a finite integrated correlation weight despite the geometric dilution of individual pair correlations.

**Method.** The study employs theoretical formulation of non-Abelian gauge networks and utilizes advanced numerical techniques like parallel-tempering simulations and replica overlap analysis.

**Model / system.** The model is a non-Abelian gauge network defined on an oriented simplicial 2-complex, where link transformations belong to a group like SU(2). The dynamics are governed by a Hamiltonian involving Wilson plaquette observables with quenched random couplings.

**Key observables.** Global compatibility ($m_P$), connected adjacent correlation weight ($I_{	ext{adj}}$), and connected replica-overlap width ($W_c$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** High-disorder phase, quenched random couplings ($J_f$), and the system size ($N$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis focuses on the SU(2) model on the complete simplicial 2-complex, and the interpretation relies on distinguishing between different types of correlation decay.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the continuous disorder-driven loss of global compatibility, the finite nature of $I_{	ext{adj}}$ in the high-disorder phase, and the vanishing of replica overlap width, confirming the non-glassy liquid state.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds by defining the gauge network and Hamiltonian, then analyzing the behavior of key observables ($m_P, I_{	ext{adj}}, W_c$) across different disorder regimes to rule out glassy freezing and establish the correlated liquid phase.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We formulate a non-Abelian theory of structural consistency for systems in which dynamical transformations reside on the links of a simplicial network. Gauge covariance follows from freedom to choose local representation frames, while plaquette holonomies quantify the incompatibility of alternative paths. For an SU(2) model on the complete simplicial $2$-complex with quenched random plaquette couplings, parallel-tempering simulations reveal a continuous disorder-driven loss of global compatibility. In the high-disorder phase, the uniform compatibility $M_P$ decreases with system size, the integrated adjacent correlation weight $\mathcal I_{\mathrm{adj}}$ remains finite value. Moreover, the connected replica-overlap width approaches the numerical noise floor and its distribution narrows, providing no evidence for thermodynamic replica-symmetry breaking. Dense frustration therefore produces a non-glassy correlated gauge liquid in which individual pair correlations are geometrically diluted while a finite integrated correlation weight survives.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper provides a comprehensive gate-level implementation framework for Lackadaisical Quantum Walk search, bridging the gap between theory and hardware. The authors detail the circuit construction, validate its performance under noise, and rigorously analyze the required quantum resources using both logical and fault-tolerant estimates. This work establishes a practical roadmap for testing advanced quantum search algorithms on superconducting quantum hardware.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work is highly relevant as it translates an abstract quantum algorithm into concrete, resource-quantified circuit blueprints, which is the necessary step for experimental realization on near-term quantum processors.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the gap between the theoretical advantages of Lackadaisical Quantum Walks (LQW) and their practical, gate-level realization for execution on current quantum hardware.

**Main result.** They provide a complete, validated gate-level circuit construction for LQW search and perform detailed resource analyses, quantifying the required qubits and gate counts for scaling up to large grids.

**Method.** The authors developed a gate-level framework by encoding the walk dynamics using oracle, coin, and flip-flop shift operations, validating the circuit under both ideal and realistic noisy superconducting hardware models.

**Model / system.** The model is the Lackadaisical Quantum Walk (LQW) on a 2D grid, which extends standard quantum walks by incorporating weighted self-loops to enhance search localization. The implementation is mapped onto a qubit register structure suitable for superconducting quantum hardware.

**Key observables.** Search success probability, algorithmic register size (9 to 15 qubits), transpiled gate count ($3.63	imes10^{5}$ to $4.38	imes10^{6}$), and circuit depth.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Self-loop weight ($\ell$), grid size ($8	imes8$ to $64	imes64$), physical error rate ($p=10^{-3}$), and code distance for surface-code analysis.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The physical resource analysis assumes a surface-code model and relies on the decomposability of multi-controlled gates into native one- and two-qubit gates.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the 1D and 2D walk structures, basic coin circuits, and the circuit components (increment/decrement) used for the position register.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from defining the LQW model and its theoretical benefits, to presenting the explicit gate-level circuit construction, validating it under noise, and concluding with detailed logical and fault-tolerant physical resource estimations.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Lackadaisical quantum walks (LQW) extend discrete-time quantum walks (DTQW) by introducing weighted self-loops, enabling improved spatial-search performance through controlled localization of the walker. Although their theoretical properties and algorithmic advantages have been studied extensively, practical gate-level realizations suitable for execution on quantum hardware remain largely unexplored. This gap limits the assessment of lackadaisical quantum walk search under realistic architectural constraints, noise processes, and resource requirements. In this work, we present a gate-level implementation framework for lackadaisical quantum walk search. The proposed construction encodes the position and coin spaces into qubit registers, and realizes the walk dynamics through oracle, coin, and flip-flop shift operations. We validate the circuit by reproducing the expected search behavior for single and multiple marked vertices and by analyzing the effect of the self-loop weight on the success probability. We further evaluate the implementation under realistic noisy settings using superconducting hardware's noise models and apply noise-mitigation techniques to improve the measured search performance. Logical-resource analysis shows that, for grids ranging from $8\times8$ to $64\times64$, the algorithmic register increases from 9 to 15 qubits, while the transpiled gate count increases from $3.63\times10^{5}$ to $4.38\times10^{6}$ and the circuit depth from $2.13\times10^{5}$ to $2.56\times10^{6}$. Finally, fault-tolerant resource estimates based on a surface-code model using the Microsoft Quantum Resource Estimator demonstrate the substantial space-time trade-off associated with magic-state production.

</details>

**Summary.** This work uses quantum Monte Carlo simulations on quantum magnets to study confinement in the Valence Bond Solid (VBS) phase near a quantum critical point. By introducing controlled spinon defects, the authors provide energetic and real-space evidence for string breaking, demonstrating that the complex organization of these spinon complexes is governed by both quantum numbers and underlying lattice geometry.

**Why it may be interesting.** The work provides a concrete, measurable analogy to gauge theories (like QCD) using condensed matter systems, offering insights into confinement mechanisms that are highly relevant to strongly correlated electron physics.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates the nature of confinement and gauge-constrained structures, specifically string breaking, in quantum magnets near a Deconfined Quantum Critical Point (DQCP).

**Main result.** String breaking is confirmed by the saturation of the excitation energy ($\Delta E$) at large separations, and the analysis establishes pinned defects as a controllable tool for resolving multi-spinon structures in a VBS phase.

**Method.** The study employs Quantum Monte Carlo (QMC) simulations and analyzes the energy scaling of defect excitations using phenomenological fitting forms.

**Model / system.** The system is a quantum magnet modeled near a DQCP, exhibiting a Valence Bond Solid (VBS) phase characterized by a $	ext{Z}_4$ structure. The physics is analyzed by introducing controlled static spinon sources via pinned-spin defects.

**Key observables.** Defect excitation energies ($\Delta E$), VBS angle texture ($\phi_{	ext{VBS}}$), and the winding number ($q_C$) of the domain walls.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The critical separation distance ($d$), the lattice size ($L=48$), and the VBS phase offsets are crucial parameters.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on approximating the energy dependence using a phenomenological form $\Delta E(d) = \Delta_\infty + \sigma d + \alpha/d$, and assumes the VBS domain walls are weakly locked by a $	ext{Z}_4$ anisotropy.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the spinon texture (winding of $\phi_{	ext{VBS}}$), the energy crossover showing string breaking (Fig. 2), and the formation of robust, connected domain-wall networks (Fig. 4).

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by establishing the physical model (VBS phase near DQCP), analyzing the energy scaling of simple dipoles (two-pin), confirming string breaking, and then extending the analysis to complex, connected multi-spinon complexes (four-pin) and domain-wall networks.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Quantum magnets provide a microscopic platform for studying confinement and gauge-constrained structures mediated by emergent gauge fields. We investigate confined spinon complexes in the columnar valence-bond-solid (VBS) phase near deconfined quantum criticality using pinned-spin defects and quantum Monte Carlo simulations. The pinned spins act as static spinon sources with controlled positions, spin projections, and VBS vorticities, enabling measurement of defect energies and direct real-space visualization of the associated VBS domain-wall strings. For matched spinon-antispinon sources, the excitation energy saturates beyond a characteristic separation as one extended dipole reorganizes into two shorter neutral dipoles, providing energetic and real-space evidence of string breaking. We further show that domain-wall connectivity is governed by lattice-scale VBS phase offsets in addition to vorticity and spin-projection neutrality. Compatible multi-pin patterns generate connected four-spinon complexes and extended domain-wall networks that retain their global connectivity under local distortions. These results establish pinned-spin defects as a controlled tool for assembling and resolving multi-spinon structures and their confining strings in a VBS phase.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper theoretically investigates how disorder drives the metal-insulator transition in correlated kagome systems using the DQMC method. It demonstrates that disorder cooperatively localizes electrons, leading to a phase diagram distinguishing between different insulating states. The findings offer actionable insights for controlling electronic properties in kagome-based materials for device applications.

**Why it may be interesting.** The study provides a detailed theoretical framework for understanding how external perturbations (disorder) can control fundamental quantum phase transitions (MIT) in strongly correlated lattice models, which is relevant for designing novel quantum materials.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates the metal-insulator transition (MIT) in correlated kagome systems, focusing specifically on the role and effect of introducing hopping disorder.

**Main result.** The interplay between disorder and electron correlations promotes electron localization, leading to distinct insulating phases (Anderson vs. Mott) and allowing disorder to tune the MIT critical parameters.

**Method.** The research employs the Determinant Quantum Monte Carlo (DQMC) method to calculate electronic transport and magnetic properties for the disordered Hubbard model.

**Model / system.** The system is a correlated electron model on a kagome lattice, described by a disordered Hubbard Hamiltonian. Disorder is introduced into the hopping terms ($t_{ij}$), and correlations are governed by the on-site Coulomb repulsion ($U$).

**Key observables.** DC conductivity ($\sigma_{dc}(T)$), kinetic energy ($\langle K angle$), density of states at the Fermi level ($N(0)$), and real-space spin-spin correlation functions ($\langle \mathbf{S}_{\alphaeta}(\mathbf{r}) angle$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Disorder strength ($\Delta$), Coulomb interaction strength ($U$), and temperature ($T$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The DQMC method is used as a nonperturbative approach, and the calculation of $\sigma_{dc}(T)$ is assumed accurate at low temperatures.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 presents a phase diagram distinguishing between AFM metal, correlated Anderson insulator, and disordered Mott insulator. Other figures track $\sigma_{dc}(T)$, $\langle 	ext{sign} angle_T$, and magnetic correlations as functions of $U$ and $\Delta$.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically analyzes the system by calculating key observables ($\sigma_{dc}$, $\langle K angle$, correlations) using DQMC. It then maps out the phase diagram by varying disorder ($\Delta$) and interaction strength ($U$), concluding with a summary of the distinct insulating regimes.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

The metal-insulator transition is often accompanied by fascinating quantum phenomena, including superconducting domes, antiferromagnetic phase transitions, and quantum spin liquids. Concurrently, kagome materials are predominantly metallic, necessitating the realization of insulating states to fully exploit their significant potential in logic and optoelectronic device applications. To address this, we investigate the electronic transport and magnetic properties in correlated kagome systems with hopping disorder using the determinant quantum Monte Carlo method. Through comprehensive analysis of the kinetic energy, dc conductivity, and density of states at the Fermi level, we demonstrate that the cooperative interplay between hopping disorder and electron correlations promotes electron localization. Within the insulator, an increase in the disorder level reduces the Coulomb interaction required for the Mott transition. Additionally, while disorder partially suppresses antiferromagnetic ordering, it remains insufficient to induce a complete magnetic transition. Finally, we summarize two schematic regions distinguishing between antiferromagnetic metal, correlated Anderson insulator, and disordered Mott insulator. Our study advances the understanding of metal-insulator transition in kagome systems by disorder and provides actionable insights for experimental control of these transitions.

</details>
