**Summary.** This theoretical paper analyzes how Majorana tetron qubits lose quantum information due to coupling with external quasiparticles. Using the Bloch-Redfield equation, the authors derive key rates for decoherence and parity leakage. The results are crucial for understanding and improving the time-domain measurements of Majorana qubits in quantum computing prototypes.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses decoherence mechanisms in solid-state qubits, providing quantitative tools to predict experimental limitations in topological quantum computing platforms.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates the dissipative dynamics of a Majorana tetron qubit when subjected to environmental noise from quasiparticle poisoning.

**Main result.** The analysis yields analytical expressions for the steady state, parity leakage, and decoherence rates, showing how the dephasing rate's exponential suppression is gradually removed by the qubit's energy splitting.

**Method.** The study employs the Bloch-Redfield equation, derived using the Born-Markov approximation, to model the open quantum system dynamics.

**Model / system.** The system is a Majorana tetron qubit, modeled as a superconducting island hosting four Majorana zero modes, coupled to external leads (the bath). The dynamics are governed by a master equation derived from the total Hamiltonian.

**Key observables.** Steady state, parity leakage rate, and decoherence rate of the Majorana qubit.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Charging energy (U), direct couplings ($\epsilon$), and coupling strength ($\Gamma_0$). Key regimes include the degenerate ($\epsilon \ll \Gamma_0$) and secular ($\epsilon \gg \Gamma_0$) limits.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The Born-Markov approximation is used, requiring the bath correlation time to be much shorter than the system evolution time ($	au_D \gg 	au_B$).

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 schematically shows the tetron island coupled to four fermionic leads, and Figure 2 illustrates the energy-level scheme of the isolated Majorana system.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from defining the full Hamiltonian and the open system dynamics via the Bloch-Redfield equation, deriving the master equation under approximations, and finally analyzing the resulting rates in different energy regimes.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We study the dissipative dynamics of a Majorana tetron qubit in the presence of extrinsic quasiparticle poisoning due to the coupling to external leads. From the Bloch-Redfield equation describing a finite-size topological superconductor hosting four Majorana zero modes, we recover analytical expressions for the steady state, the parity leakage rate, and the decoherence rate of a Majorana qubit at arbitrary values of the charging energy. The analysis shows that the exponential suppression of the dephasing rate is gradually removed by the energy splitting of the qubit states. These results can be useful to understand time-domain experimental data in Majorana qubit prototypes.

</details>

### [Implementation Possibility of Quantum Simulation for Quantum Molecular Dynamics](http://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17261v1)

**Authors:** Xingyu Zhang, Weijia Guo, Jinke Yu, Qingyong Meng  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** other · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2608.17261v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks

**Summary.** This paper investigates the theoretical possibility of using quantum simulation for quantum molecular dynamics, particularly for chemical reactions. It critically analyzes underlying mathematical requirements, focusing on issues like the conjugacy between systems and the implications of real-valued quantum mechanics. The work provides a theoretical roadmap for advancing quantum simulation in chemistry.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses the theoretical limits and necessary mathematical foundations for applying quantum computation to simulate fundamental processes in molecular physics and chemical dynamics.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper explores the theoretical feasibility of using quantum simulation techniques to model complex quantum molecular dynamics, especially for chemical reaction processes.

**Main result.** The authors analyze fundamental theoretical hurdles, such as the conjugacy relation between dynamical systems, the transition to real-valued quantum mechanics, and the emergence of geometric phases from degrees of freedom separation.

**Method.** The analysis reviews existing quantum-classical mixed simulation implementations and focuses on theoretical constraints by examining the mathematical requirements for simulating time evolution.

**Model / system.** The focus is on quantum many-body systems undergoing molecular reaction dynamics. The theoretical framework involves comparing the target molecular system's dynamics with the dynamics implemented on a quantum simulator.

**Key observables.** Population fractions ($P_0$), rate constants ($k$), and the general time evolution of the quantum state.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The separation of degrees of freedom (DOFs) and the resulting geometric phase ambiguity are key theoretical parameters.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The paper assumes the necessity of a conjugacy relation for successful simulation and discusses the theoretical implications of using real-valued quantum mechanics instead of complex Hilbert spaces.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate various quantum algorithms (VQE, qEOM) and model results, such as the time evolution of the Marcus spin-boson model showing charge transfer regimes.

**Paper structure.** The paper is structured theoretically, analyzing four key aspects (conjugacy, wave function correspondence, real-valued issues, geometric phase) by reviewing existing quantum-classical mixed implementations.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

In this work, we explore the implementation possibility of quantum simulation for quantum molecular dynamics, in particular for reaction dynamics, though several implementations have already reported through quantum-classical mixed simulations ({\it Acc. Chem. Res.} {\bf 54} (2021), 4229 and {\it J. Phys. Chem. Lett.} {\bf xx} (2026), XXXX). To analyze this aspect, we examine (1) the conjugacy relation between quantum simulator and the target molecular system, (2) the wave function correspondence in quantum algorithm and classical algorithm for multi-dimensional dynamics, (3) problems arisen from real-valued classical algorithms, and finally (4) geometric phase arisen from the separation among the degrees of freedom (DOFs). As is well known, the aforementioned first and second points play fundamental roles in quantum simulation of quantum many-body systems, and the third and fourth points are theoretical issues that might introduce problems in classical and quantum computing. In this work, we mainly focus on the third and fourth points by analysis of the first two points by reviewing previously reported quantum-classical mixed implementations of quantum simulation. We also consider gauge freedom in high-dimensional quantum molecular dynamics that has been introduced recently, and then discuss possibility of advantages and disadvantages of quantum simulation for molecular reaction dynamics.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical paper proves that a specific class of U(1)-symmetric spin-1 chains lacks nontrivial local conserved quantities. By applying advanced commutator techniques, the authors show that the continuous symmetry breaking observed in these models is structural (due to frustration-free nature) rather than being protected by a conserved order parameter. This provides deep insight into the underlying dynamics and integrability of these quantum spin systems.

**Why it may be interesting.** Proving the absence of local conserved quantities is a powerful tool for demonstrating non-integrability in quantum many-body systems, which is crucial for understanding thermalization and dynamics in condensed matter models.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper aims to rigorously prove the absence of nontrivial local conserved quantities in a specific, previously unstudied class of U(1)-symmetric spin-1 chains.

**Main result.** It establishes that for these models, any local conserved quantity with support up to half the system size must be a simple linear combination of the identity, the total magnetization, and the Hamiltonian itself.

**Method.** The authors employ Shiraishi's technique, analyzing the commutator $[Q, H]$ for potential conserved charges $Q$ and solving the resulting linear system of equations.

**Model / system.** The system is a spin-1 chain on a periodic lattice with nearest-neighbor interactions, characterized by dipolar and quadrupolar couplings. The focus is on models where certain quadrupolar couplings vanish, including the frustration-free Motzkin chain.

**Key observables.** Local conserved quantities ($Q$), the total magnetization ($S_z$), and the Hamiltonian ($H$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The size of the system ($N$) and the range of the conserved quantity support ($3 \le k \le N/2$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on extending previous techniques to handle vanishing quadrupolar couplings, which was a gap in prior literature.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically analyzes the nonexistence of conserved quantities by first examining 2-local, then 3-local, and finally ruling out higher-order local charges using commutator analysis.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We prove the absence of nontrivial local conserved quantities in a class of $U(1)$-symmetric spin-$1$ chains with nearest-neighbor interactions in which some of the quadrupolar couplings vanish, a class that is not covered by previous studies. Applying the technique of Shiraishi to these systems, we show that, for every model in this class on a periodic chain of $N$ sites, there is no $k$-local conserved quantity for any $3\le k\le N/2$. In particular, for a frustration-free spin-$1$ chain that exhibits spontaneous $U(1)$ symmetry breaking at zero temperature in one spatial dimension, we prove that every local conserved quantity with support up to half of the system size is a linear combination of the identity, the total magnetization $S^z$, and the Hamiltonian itself. This rigorously establishes that, unlike the Heisenberg ferromagnet, the model admits no local order parameter commuting with the Hamiltonian, so that its continuous symmetry breaking is enabled by the frustration-free structure rather than by a conserved order parameter. We also prove the absence of $k$-local conserved quantities for $3\le k\le N/2$ in the periodic Motzkin chain, a frustration-free spin-$1$ chain closely related to the original Motzkin chain, for which spontaneous $U(1)$ symmetry breaking at zero temperature has also been reported.

</details>

**Summary.** This work rigorously analyzes the spectral properties of a singular quantum kicked rotor by mapping it onto a nonlinear sigma model. Using advanced renormalization group techniques, the authors demonstrate that the rotor's dynamics fall into the same universality class as power-law random banded matrices. This resolves a key theoretical question regarding the relevance of deterministic correlations in the context of Anderson localization.

**Why it may be interesting.** The connection between deterministic, structured quantum dynamics (the rotor) and the universality class of random matrix theory (PRBM) provides deep insights into the role of correlations in localization phenomena, relevant for understanding disordered quantum systems.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates whether the deterministic correlations of a singular quantum kicked rotor become irrelevant at long distances, determining if it shares the same infrared theory as power-law random banded matrices (PRBM).

**Main result.** The rotor successfully reproduces the long-range Anderson transition of the corresponding PRBM in the localized and critical regimes after appropriate matching of symmetry and coupling conventions.

**Method.** The authors derive a nonlocal supersymmetric nonlinear sigma model from the rotor and perform a detailed renormalization-group (RG) analysis up to two-loop order.

**Model / system.** The system is a singular quantum kicked rotor, whose dynamics are governed by a Floquet operator. It is mathematically compared to PRBM, which are characterized by algebraically decaying momentum-space matrix elements.

**Key observables.** Inverse participation ratios (IPR), two-level quasienergy correlator, and the analysis of localization/critical/extended infrared regimes.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The singularity type (power-law or logarithmic), the RG flow parameters (stiffness $t$, local moment renormalization $Z_p$), and the symmetry class.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis requires matching the symmetry class, coupling convention, and asymptotic scaling window between the rotor and the PRBM.

**Figures summary.** Not specified.

**Paper structure.** The paper proceeds by deriving the sigma model, performing RG analysis on correlation functions (like the two-level correlator), and analyzing the flow equations for key parameters like stiffness and local moment renormalization.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Singular kicked rotors have long been compared with power-law random banded matrices (PRBM) because their momentum-space Floquet matrix elements decay algebraically. However, it has remained unclear whether the deterministic correlations of the rotor become irrelevant at long distances and, consequently, under what conditions the two systems share the same infrared theory. To address this question, we derive a nonlocal supersymmetric nonlinear sigma model directly from a quantum kicked rotor with a power-law or logarithmic singularity. By carrying out the renormalization-group analysis up to two-loop order, we show that, after matching the symmetry class and coupling convention, the rotor reproduces the long-range Anderson transition of the corresponding PRBM, including its localized, critical, and extended infrared regimes.

</details>

**Summary.** This work introduces a novel method using the a.c. elastocaloric effect to map the absolute entropy and specific heat of quantum materials like $	ext{Sr}_2	ext{RuO}_4$ under uniaxial strain. By combining low- and high-frequency measurements, the authors overcome limitations of traditional probes. The results provide quantitative evidence regarding entropy changes across superconducting and magnetic phase transitions in correlated electron systems.

**Why it may be interesting.** The ability to probe absolute thermodynamic quantities like entropy and specific heat deep within the superconducting state using strain-induced effects provides a powerful, non-invasive tool for characterizing strongly correlated electron systems.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To develop a quantitative framework to reconstruct absolute entropy and specific heat across complex phase diagrams of quantum materials under uniaxial pressure, overcoming limitations of conventional thermodynamic probes.

**Main result.** The method successfully maps the absolute entropy and specific heat for $	ext{Sr}_2	ext{RuO}_4$, showing that entropy quenching is strongest at the critical strain corresponding to the Van Hove singularity.

**Method.** The analysis combines measurements from the low-frequency (strong coupling) and high-frequency (quasi-adiabatic) regimes of the a.c. elastocaloric effect to achieve absolute accuracy in entropy reconstruction.

**Model / system.** The study focuses on the correlated superconductor $	ext{Sr}_2	ext{RuO}_4$ subjected to uniaxial strain. The theoretical framework uses the entropy balance equation to relate heat flow from an external heater to the elastocaloric response.

**Key observables.** Absolute entropy map, specific heat ($c/T$), and the temperature oscillation amplitude ($	ilde{T}_s$) as a function of strain and frequency.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Uniaxial strain ($\varepsilon$), temperature ($T$), and measurement frequency ($\omega$), with characteristic time scales set by $	au_1 = C_{	ext{tot}}/k$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The reconstruction relies on combining data from two distinct frequency regimes (low vs. high) and treating the elastocaloric response as an effective heat source in the thermodynamic balance equation.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the phase diagram of $	ext{Sr}_2	ext{RuO}_4$ under strain, the experimental setup, and plots comparing the normalized temperature oscillation signal ($	ilde{T}_s$) across different frequencies.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops the theoretical framework by deriving the governing differential equation unifying heating and elastocaloric effects. It then applies this framework to $	ext{Sr}_2	ext{RuO}_4$ to map the entropy and specific heat across its superconducting and magnetic phase boundaries.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Uniaxial pressure is a powerful tuning parameter for quantum materials, but conventional thermodynamic probes such as specific heat are difficult to realize in the constrained geometries of strain apparatus. We develop a quantitative analysis framework for a.c. elastocaloric effect measurements that enable the reconstruction of the absolute entropy and hence specific heat across complex phase diagrams. The absolute accuracy is achieved by combining measurements in the strong coupling regime at low frequencies with high signal-to-noise measurements in the quasi-adiabatic regime at high frequencies. Applying the approach to the correlated superconductor Sr$_2$RuO$_4$, we obtain an absolute entropy map across the phase diagram including across phase transitions deep into the superconducting state. We demonstrate that from such data one can derive the absolute specific heat which is currently not possible through other approaches. This data reinforces the finding that the quenching of entropy within the superconductor Sr$_2$RuO$_4$ is strongest at the critical strain consistent with the superconducting gap being maximized at the Van Hove singularity (VHs). Furthermore, we demonstrate that, although $Δc /(γT) $ does increase at the VH strain, this increase is much weaker than previously inferred from more indirect caloric experiments.

</details>

### [Weak Typicality of von Neumann Entanglement Entropy in Gaussian Boson Sampling](http://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17274v1)

**Authors:** Hongru Zhao  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** quantum information and computing · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2608.17274v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks
**Topic relevance:** `analog quantum simulation` **1/5** · `interference shaping light` **1/5**

<details open><summary>📷 Fig 1</summary>

<img src="2608.17274_figures/2608.17274_fig1.jpg" width="500"><br>
<sub>Figure 1: Proof pipeline, read across the upper row and then back across the lower row. Here U is the Haar distributed interferometer, BU is the selected principal block of UUT, τj are its singular values, νj are the resulting symplectic eigenvalues, S1,n is the von Neumann entropy, and Fn,η is its endpoint regularized approximation. See Eqs. (10), (11), (18), (21), and (29). Haar concentration is applied to Fn,η.</sub>

</details>

**Summary.** This paper rigorously proves the weak typicality of entanglement entropy in Gaussian Boson Sampling models. It shows that the measured entropy is highly concentrated around its expected value, establishing a quantitative concentration bound. This result is significant for understanding the typical scaling laws governing entanglement in large-scale quantum optical devices.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides rigorous mathematical tools for analyzing entanglement measures in complex quantum optical circuits, which is crucial for understanding the resource scaling and fundamental limits of photonic quantum computation.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates the weak typicality of the von Neumann entanglement entropy generated by a Haar distributed passive interferometer acting on squeezed input modes.

**Main result.** It proves that the ratio of the entanglement entropy to its expectation value converges in probability to 1, establishing proportional weak typicality for this specific Gaussian Boson Sampling setup.

**Method.** The proof involves representing the entropy as a singular value statistic of a principal block of the interferometer's unitary matrix and applying concentration inequalities on the unitary group.

**Model / system.** The system is Gaussian Boson Sampling (GBS) using an ideal, lossless interferometer acting on $n$ equally squeezed input modes. The evolution is governed by a Haar distributed unitary $U$.

**Key observables.** Von Neumann entanglement entropy ($S_{1,n}$), the ratio $S_{1,n} / \mathbb{E}S_{1,n}$, and the variance $	ext{Var}(S_{1,n})$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Fixed nonzero squeezing strength ($s$), and a proportional bipartition ratio ($k_n/n 	o r \in (0, 1)$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The result assumes fixed equal squeezing and a proportional bipartition; it does not cover unequal squeezing or vanishing squeezing.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the proof pipeline, connecting the unitary $U$ to its principal block $B_U$ and the singular values used in the entropy calculation.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the mathematical framework by relating the entropy to singular values, proves the weak typicality bound using concentration on the unitary group, and concludes by deriving the implications for convergence and variance.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We study the von Neumann entanglement entropy generated by a Haar distributed passive interferometer acting on $n$ equally squeezed input modes with fixed nonzero squeezing strength $s$. Previous work established proportional weak typicality for integer R'enyi orders $α\geq 2$ and stated a sublinear von Neumann result, while the proportional von Neumann case remained open. For a subsystem of $k_n$ modes satisfying $k_n/n\to r\in(0,1)$, we prove that, for every $\varepsilon>0$ and all sufficiently large $n$, $\mathbb{P}\left(\left|\frac{S_{1,n}}{\mathbb{E}S_{1,n}}-1\right|\geq\varepsilon\right)\leq2\exp\left[-\frac{c_{s,r}\varepsilon^2n^2}{\log^2(en)}\right].$ The proof represents the entropy as a singular value statistic of a principal block of $UU^{\mathsf T}$, where $U$ denotes the unitary interferometer. It regularizes the logarithmic singularity at the endpoint corresponding to a pure Gaussian mode and applies concentration on the unitary group. The result establishes proportional von Neumann weak typicality and further implies almost sure convergence of $S_{1,n}/\mathbb{E}S_{1,n}$ to $1$, a typical volume law, and the variance bound $\mathrm{Var}(S_{1,n})=O_s(\log^2 n)$. An accompanying Lean 4 development verifies the proof chain.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper introduces AutoQSense, an RL framework that automates the design of quantum sensing protocols by optimizing the circuit architecture itself, not just the parameters. By treating circuit design as a sequential decision process, it achieves superior sensitivity compared to fixed ansätze. This establishes a resource-aware methodology for adaptive quantum metrology applicable to large, complex quantum hardware.

**Why it may be interesting.** The integration of RL for circuit design directly addresses the resource-aware optimization needed in quantum metrology, providing a systematic way to navigate the vast space of possible quantum circuits.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The core problem is automating Variational Quantum Sensing (VQS) by optimizing not only continuous variational parameters but also the underlying quantum circuit architectures themselves.

**Main result.** The proposed framework, AutoQSense, successfully learns adaptive, hardware-compatible quantum sensing protocols that outperform fixed, hardware-efficient ansätze while using fewer entangling gates.

**Method.** It employs a nested optimization loop: an inner VQS loop optimizes parameters using quantum gradients, while an outer Reinforcement Learning (RL) loop updates the circuit architecture (the policy) based on the resulting Fisher Information.

**Model / system.** The system is an $n$-qubit quantum processor used for parameter estimation. The methodology scales from single-agent search for small systems to a distributed multi-agent formulation for large, lattice-connected systems.

**Key observables.** Quantum Fisher Information (QFI) and Classical Fisher Information (CFI) are the primary objectives, used to define the RL reward function.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The optimization involves variational parameters ($	heta$) and the discrete architectural choices (gate sequences/connectivity $A, B$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The primary assumption addressed is that existing VQS methods are limited by fixed, a priori circuit ansätze, which the RL framework overcomes.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the AUTOQSENSE workflow, comparing its performance against benchmarks like the Standard Quantum Limit (SQL) and fixed ansätze, especially showing gains in QFI and reduced gate count.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the VQS problem, identifies the architectural limitation, proposes the RL framework (AutoQSense), details the single-agent and multi-agent implementations, and validates the approach by showing performance gains and structural insights.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Variational quantum sensing offers a promising route to high-precision parameter estimation, but its performance depends strongly on the circuit architectures used for probe preparation and measurement. Existing approaches typically optimize continuous parameters within predefined ansätze, restricting the accessible design space and limiting adaptation to sensing tasks and hardware constraints. Here, we introduce \textsc{AutoQSense}, a reinforcement-learning framework that searches circuit architectures using Fisher-information-based objectives. For few-qubit systems, a single agent sequentially constructs preparation and measurement circuits. For larger systems, a distributed formulation assigns local circuit design to subsystem agents and inter-block entanglement to a budgeted agent. Numerical results show that the learned architectures recover known benchmark strategies, adapt to dephasing noise, and outperform fixed hardware-efficient ansätze while using fewer entangling gates. These results establish \textsc{AutoQSense} as a resource-aware approach to adaptive and hardware-compatible quantum sensing.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper introduces CLOPSh, a new benchmark metric designed to accurately measure the sustained operational speed of large-scale quantum processors. It addresses the gap in current metrics by defining speed based on layered, hardware-aware circuits, ensuring that speed measurements are intrinsically linked to independently verified quality levels. This provides a necessary, holistic tool for assessing the true utility potential of quantum hardware.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a rigorous, multi-level framework for benchmarking quantum hardware performance, which is crucial for any theoretical work relying on realistic estimates of quantum computation time or resource scaling.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Existing quantum speed metrics fail to capture the sustained, end-to-end throughput required for utility-scale quantum algorithms, especially those involving classical-quantum feedback loops.

**Main result.** The authors propose CLOPSh (Circuit Layer Operations Per Second), a holistic benchmark that measures sustained throughput by defining speed over hardware-aware, layered circuits, thereby linking speed directly to independently verified quality envelopes.

**Method.** The work formalizes a new benchmarking metric, CLOPSh, which measures the rate of executing physical circuit layers, ensuring the speed measurement is structurally compatible with existing Layer Fidelity (LF) quality benchmarks.

**Model / system.** The focus is on scaling quantum processors to hundreds of qubits, analyzing performance across an abstraction hierarchy (Qubit $ightarrow$ Device $ightarrow$ System $ightarrow$ Application) to address utility-scale workloads.

**Key observables.** CLOPSh (Circuit Layer Operations Per Second), Max Circuits Per Second (MCPS), Layer Fidelity (LF), and the overall system throughput rate.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Utility-scale workloads, layered/parameterized circuits, and the need to account for classical-quantum overheads.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The primary assumption is that sustained, steady-state throughput, measured layer-by-layer, is the correct metric to guide hardware development beyond simple gate counts or fidelity measurements.

**Figures summary.** The notes mention a table organizing representative benchmarks across four levels of abstraction (Qubit, Device, System, Application) for Scale, Quality, and Speed, and a figure illustrating physical sublayers as the counting unit for CLOPSh.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the limitations of existing metrics (QV, gate rates), introduces the comprehensive CLOPS framework, and then details CLOPSh as the specific, necessary instantiation for the utility-scale regime, ensuring speed and quality are measured coherently.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

As quantum processors scale to hundreds of qubits, execution speed is a critical performance dimension alongside scale and quality. While substantial progress has been made in benchmarking circuit fidelity, existing speed metrics often fail to reflect the sustained, end-to-end throughput experienced by users running utility-scale workloads. This shortfall is especially pronounced for layered, parameterized circuits executed repeatedly within classical-quantum workflows, such as variational algorithms and error-mitigated simulations.   In this work, we formalize CLOPS_h (Circuit Layer Operations Per Second) as a holistic speed benchmark defined over layered, hardware-aware circuits. CLOPS_h measures the sustained rate at which the system executes physical layers, parallel slices of qubit-disjoint two-qubit gates separated by synchronization barriers. Because each such layer is one time slice of an N-qubit circuit, this rate maps directly to the execution rate of layered $N$-qubit circuits, connecting CLOPS_h to published device capability claims, and to the device-level throughput ceiling we formalize as Max Circuits Per Second (MCPS). CLOPS_h is obtained under layer-fidelity operating conditions, binding the speed measurement to an independently verified quality envelope, and it shares its layer decomposition with scalable layer-fidelity (LF) quality benchmarks, enabling coherent interpretation of speed and quality without conflating the two.

</details>

**Summary.** This work introduces Entanglement-Weighted Pruning (EWP), a novel unlearning technique for quantum federated learning. EWP prunes quantum circuit parameters by combining data sensitivity (via QFIM) and structural importance (via entanglement weight). It demonstrates that this method achieves near-perfect data removal accuracy while drastically reducing computational overhead compared to full model retraining.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a concrete, quantum-enhanced technique for model auditing and data removal, which is crucial for deploying quantum machine learning models in regulated, multi-party settings.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the challenge of 'machine unlearning' in Quantum Federated Learning (QFL) to efficiently remove the influence of a specific client's data contribution, satisfying data privacy regulations like GDPR.

**Main result.** The proposed Entanglement-Weighted Pruning (EWP) method achieved post-unlearning accuracy statistically indistinguishable from full retraining while requiring significantly less computational time.

**Method.** EWP scores each trainable circuit parameter by multiplying the diagonal entry of the Quantum Fisher Information Matrix (QFIM) by a structural entanglement weight derived from the gate's coupling.

**Model / system.** The model uses variational quantum classifiers trained on a four-qubit data-re-uploading ansatz within a federated learning framework (FedAvg).

**Key observables.** Classification accuracy, AUROC, forgetting score, membership-advantage gap, and wall-clock time.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The trainable circuit parameters ($	heta_k$), the QFIM diagonal entries ($F_{kk}$), and the structural entanglement weight ($w_{ent}(k)$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The method relies on the diagonal approximation of the QFIM and assumes that parameters with low combined scores can be safely pruned without significant performance loss.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the end-to-end EWP pipeline across six stages, while Figure 2 details the federated communication workflow during training and forgetting.

**Paper structure.** The paper outlines the QFL setup, introduces the EWP mechanism combining QFIM and entanglement weights, details the pruning process, and benchmarks EWP against various baselines using simulated supply-chain risk data.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Federated deployments of variational quantum classifiers are attractive for cross-organisation risk prediction in supply chains, because raw data never leaves the client, yet data-protection regulations such as the GDPR grant clients a right to request that their contribution be removed from a trained model after the fact. Retraining a federated model from scratch to honour such a request is correct but wasteful, and it is not obvious which quantum circuit parameters actually carry a given client's influence. We introduce Entanglement-Weighted Pruning (EWP), an unlearning procedure for quantum federated learning that scores every trainable circuit parameter with the product of two signals: the diagonal entry of the quantum Fisher information matrix estimated on the target client's data via the parameter-shift rule, and a structural entanglement weight associated with the parameter's gate. Parameters with the lowest scores are pruned, optionally followed by a short fine-tuning pass on the retained clients. We implement the full pipeline in Qiskit for a four-qubit data-re-uploading ansatz trained with FedAvg across five simulated supply-chain-risk clients, and benchmark EWP against full retraining, fine-tuning alone, random pruning, Fisher-only pruning, and entanglement-only pruning, over three random seeds. EWP attains a mean post-unlearning accuracy statistically indistinguishable from the full-retraining oracle, while producing a lower forgetting score and requiring roughly 16 times less wall-clock time. Ablations over pruning threshold, client count, and non-IID strength show that combining the two signals is necessary, as entanglement-only and Fisher-only pruning each substantially degrade accuracy relative to EWP.

</details>

### [Exact Moments of Gaussian Gram Hafnians Reveal an $n^2/\log n$ Threshold for Weak Anticoncentration](http://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17065v1)

**Authors:** Hongru Zhao  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** quantum information and computing · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2608.17065v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks
**Topic relevance:** `analog quantum simulation` **1/5**

<details open><summary>📷 Fig 1</summary>

<img src="2608.17065_figures/2608.17065_fig1.jpg" width="500"><br>
<sub>Figure 1: Exact finite sum ratio from Eq. (4) for n = 10, 30, 100, together with the Bessel limit in Eq. (7). Both axes are logarithmic; symbols show exact finite n evaluations, not simulated data, and the vertical line marks k = n2. For every fixed k/n2 &gt; 0, the scaled ratio approaches a positive limit, so the quadratic scale is a crossover within the weak regime.</sub>

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical paper analyzes the moments of the Gaussian Gram Hafnian, a key observable in Gaussian Boson Sampling. By calculating the moment ratio $R_{k,n}$, the authors establish that the threshold for weak anticoncentration scales as $k \sim n^2 / \log n$. This provides a precise mathematical boundary for the performance limits of the surrogate model used to study quantum interference effects.

**Why it may be interesting.** The connection between moment calculations of quantum observables (like the Hafnian) and establishing fundamental scaling thresholds (like the $n^2/\log n$ boundary) is highly relevant for understanding the limits of quantum advantage in photonic quantum information processing.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper determines the scaling threshold for 'weak anticoncentration' by analyzing the exact moments of Gaussian Gram Hafnians.

**Main result.** The critical scaling order boundary for weak anticoncentration is found to be $k \sim n^2 / \log n$, contrasting with the smooth Bessel crossover observed at $k \sim n^2$.

**Method.** The analysis involves calculating the second and fourth moments of the Hafnian, $H_{k,n}$, by reducing them to rank two Gaussian integrals and expressing the resulting moment ratio in terms of terminating generalized hypergeometric polynomials.

**Model / system.** The study uses the independent Gaussian surrogate model for collision-free Gaussian Boson Sampling (GBS). The core mathematical object is the Gram Hafnian, $H_{k,n} = 	ext{haf}(X^T X)$, where $X$ has independent standard circular complex Gaussian entries.

**Key observables.** The moment ratio $R_{k,n} = (\mathbb{E}|H_{k,n}|^2)^2 / \mathbb{E}|H_{k,n}|^4$ is the primary diagnostic quantity.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The ratio $k/n^2$ (squeezed input modes to detected photons) and the logarithmic scaling factor $\log n$ are crucial parameters.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The results are strictly limited to the independent Gaussian surrogate moment criterion and do not apply to finite dimensional Haar moment transfer or high probability small ball anticoncentration.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 compares the exact finite sum ratio $R_{k,n}$ against the theoretical Bessel limit, marking the $k=n^2$ crossover line.

**Paper structure.** The paper proceeds by deriving exact moment formulas for the Hafnian, establishing the closed-form expression for the moment ratio $R_{k,n}$ using hypergeometric functions, and finally analyzing the asymptotic behavior to pinpoint the $n^2/\log n$ threshold for weak anticoncentration.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Anticoncentration is central to hardness arguments for approximate sampling. In the independent Gaussian surrogate for collision free Gaussian boson sampling, the moment ratio studied here also determines the averaged ideal linear cross entropy reference value. Let $H_{k,n}=\mathrm{haf}(X^{\mathsf T}X)$, where $X\in\mathbb{C}^{k\times 2n}$ has independent standard circular complex Gaussian entries. We evaluate $\mathbb{E}|H_{k,n}|^2$ and $\mathbb{E}|H_{k,n}|^4$ exactly by reducing four hafnian copies to a rank two Gaussian integral. For $R_{k,n}=(\mathbb{E}|H_{k,n}|^2)^2/\mathbb{E}|H_{k,n}|^4$, we obtain $R_{k,n}=4^{-n}\binom{2n}{n}/F_{k,n}$, where $F_{k,n}={}3F_2(-n,-n,1/2;1,k/2;1)$ is a terminating generalized hypergeometric polynomial. If $k/n^2\to c>0$, then $F{k,n}\to e^{1/c}I_0(1/c)$, where $I_0$ is the modified Bessel function of the first kind of order zero, and consequently $R_{k,n}\sqrt{πn}\to[e^{1/c}I_0(1/c)]^{-1}$. Thus $k\asymp n^2$ is a smooth Bessel crossover, whereas the scaling order boundary for inverse polynomial weak anticoncentration is $k\asymp n^2/\log n$. These conclusions concern the Gaussian surrogate moment criterion; finite dimensional Haar moment transfer and high probability small ball anticoncentration remain separate problems.

</details>

**Summary.** This work develops a framework for random walks on simplicial complexes, modeling transport via two coupled channels: vertex-mediated and face-mediated. By analyzing the effect of partially retained triangles, the authors demonstrate that intermediate levels of structural disorder create a pronounced transport bottleneck. This establishes a simple yet powerful method to quantify how higher-order connectivity reshapes network dynamics.

**Why it may be interesting.** The study of transport bottlenecks due to structural heterogeneity is highly relevant to understanding energy or information flow in complex, disordered physical systems, which shares conceptual links with glassy dynamics or disordered quantum systems.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates how incorporating heterogeneous higher-order connectivity (triangles) into a random walk fundamentally alters its spectral and transport properties compared to simple pairwise network dynamics.

**Main result.** The analysis reveals that the most pronounced transport bottleneck—manifested as maximum mean first-passage time—occurs in an intermediate regime of partial face dilution, suggesting heterogeneity is key.

**Method.** The authors analyze the effective transition operator governing the random walk on the edges of a simplicial complex, using spectral analysis and calculating mean first-passage times.

**Model / system.** The model is an incidence-based random walk defined on the edges of a random 2D simplicial complex. The dynamics combine two channels: one mediated by vertices (lower) and one by triangular faces (upper), controlled by a mixing parameter q.

**Key observables.** Second-largest eigenvalue modulus, inverse participation ratio, mean first-passage time, and the survival probability of first-passage time.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Face retention probability (p) and the mixing parameter (q), which control the degree of higher-order connectivity.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes the underlying 1-skeleton is fixed (complete graph) and uses a random ensemble approach to study quenched structural heterogeneity.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the dependence of mean first-passage time on face density (p) and the survival probability of first-passage times across different dilution regimes.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds the effective transition operator by combining elementary steps via vertices and triangles. It then analyzes the resulting dynamics across different regimes (graph-like vs. fully simplicial) to pinpoint the conditions causing dynamical slowdown.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We introduce an incidence-based random walk on the edges of a random two-dimensional simplicial complex with a complete $1$-skeleton and independently retained triangular faces. The dynamics combine two transport channels, one mediated by vertices and the other by triangular faces, through an effective transition operator controlled by a mixing parameter $q$. This construction isolates the effects of higher-order connectivity without modifying the underlying pairwise support of the walk. We characterize the model through structural observables, spectral relaxation, stationary localization, and first-passage transport. Our results show that partial face retention generates heterogeneous higher-order connectivity, giving rise to a pronounced transport bottleneck at intermediate face densities. In this regime, the second-largest eigenvalue modulus, the inverse participation ratio of the stationary distribution, and the mean first-passage time all exhibit non-monotonic behavior, reaching their largest values at intermediate face densities. The corresponding first-passage-time distributions reveal an enhanced probability of unusually long trajectories. Together, these results establish a simple framework for investigating how heterogeneous higher-order connectivity reshapes spectral and transport properties beyond pairwise network dynamics.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper introduces Iterative Tensor Network Transformations (ITNT), a novel framework allowing general nonlinear functions to be evaluated directly on data compressed in the Tensor Train format. By leveraging iterative root-finding techniques, ITNT achieves high accuracy for complex physical simulations, such as calculating reaction rates in fluid dynamics and solving NP-hard optimization problems. This establishes tensor networks as a tool for general-purpose, large-scale data science.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a powerful, general-purpose computational tool that extends tensor network methods beyond linear operations, making them applicable to complex, nonlinear physics problems previously requiring computationally expensive full-space sampling.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The core challenge is developing a general algorithmic framework to perform element-wise evaluation of arbitrary, nonlinear filtering functions directly on data encoded in the compressed Tensor Train (TT) format.

**Main result.** The proposed Iterative Tensor Network Transformations (ITNT) successfully enables high-fidelity computation of nonlinear functions (like reaction rates) and locating global extrema in exponentially large configuration spaces.

**Method.** ITNT utilizes iterative techniques, such as the Newton-Raphson method, combined with efficient TT operations (element-wise product, partial integration) to operate entirely in the compressed domain.

**Model / system.** The methods are demonstrated on several models: 3D reactive flow fields (methane/air jet flame) for continuous field evaluation, and classical Ising models/Max-SAT instances for combinatorial optimization.

**Key observables.** Reaction rates (Arrhenius factor), global energy extrema ($E_{opt}$), and the relative $\ell_2$ error ($	ilde{\epsilon}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Bond dimension ($\chi$), number of iterations ($N_{	ext{iter}}$), and the required preprocessing steps (e.g., self-multiplication power $P$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The framework assumes that the required functions can be mapped to iterative root-finding problems solvable via the Newton-Raphson method or related fixed-point iterations.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the methodology for nonlinear evaluation (Fig. 1a, 1b) and the application to optimization, showing convergence comparisons between ITNT and interpolation methods (TCI).

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces ITNT, details its application to continuous nonlinear PDEs (reactive flow), and then extends it to discrete, high-dimensional optimization problems like Max-SAT and Ising model energy matching.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Tensor networks are powerful formats for compressing large-scale data. However, their application to general data processing has been limited by the difficulty of performing nonlinear operations. Here, we introduce iterative tensor network transformations (ITNTs), a general algorithmic framework for the element-wise evaluation of elementary and nonlinear filtering functions on data encoded as tensor trains (TTs), a class of tensor networks. Our approach operates entirely in the compressed domain, enabling efficient computation on exponentially large datasets while maintaining a controlled computational cost. We demonstrate its power in two key areas: (I) evaluating highly nonlinear elementary and filtering functions on a 3D reactive flow field, enabling high-fidelity reaction rate computation and region filtering, and (II) finding extrema in complex optimization problems, such as solving Max-SAT instances on spaces up to $2^{70}$ configurations. These results establish ITNT as a foundational tool that provides tensor network methods with the capability for general-purpose data science and large-scale optimization.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper proposes QDAGer, a quantum-inspired graph Transformer, to enhance graph learning by incorporating dynamical features derived from the Transverse-Field Ising Model. The authors show that the time evolution of quantum observables acts as a powerful structural signal, enabling the model to learn complex metrics like Graph Edit Distance better than purely classical structural descriptors.

**Why it may be interesting.** The work bridges quantum many-body physics (Ising models, dynamics) with modern machine learning (Graph Transformers), suggesting that inherent symmetries and time-evolution properties of quantum systems can serve as powerful, structured inductive biases for solving classically hard problems.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To develop graph representations for graph learning that capture structural information while respecting permutation invariance, particularly for hard tasks like graph comparison.

**Main result.** The proposed quantum-inspired architecture, QDAGer, demonstrates that dynamical features derived from the Ising model provide a stronger inductive bias for learning Graph Edit Distance than classical structural features.

**Method.** The authors introduce QDAGer, a Transformer that injects time-series data from quantum observables (node occupations and two-point correlators) into the attention mechanism for graph-pair comparison.

**Model / system.** The underlying physical model is the quantum evolution governed by a graph-indexed Transverse-Field Ising Hamiltonian ($H_G$). The dynamics are analyzed by measuring time-dependent expectation values of local observables.

**Key observables.** Node occupations $\langle \hat{
u}_u(t) angle$ and connected two-point correlators $\langle \hat{
u}_u \hat{
u}_v(t) angle - \langle \hat{
u}_u(t) angle \langle \hat{
u}_v(t) angle$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The performance is tested across various Graph Edit Distance (GED) benchmarks using different edit cost settings.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The quantum dynamics are simulated via emulation, and the method is positioned as 'quantum-inspired' rather than claiming quantum computational advantage.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 (from Chunk 2 notes) shows that one-body occupation trajectories differ between non-isomorphic graphs (Rook's vs Shrikhande), demonstrating distinguishability.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the theoretical distinguishability of non-isomorphic graphs using the Ising model dynamics (Chunks 2 & 3), proving that observables yield distinct time-dependent trajectories. It then applies this concept by proposing QDAGer, which integrates these dynamical features into a Transformer for practical graph learning tasks like GED (Chunks 1 & 4).

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We study the quantum evolution induced by graph-indexed Ising Hamiltonians as a source of structural signal for graph learning. Graph automorphisms preserve symmetries of the Hamiltonian, and these symmetries constrain the quantum evolution in a way that turns time-dependent local measurements into informative probes of graph structure. Leveraging this idea, we introduce QDAGer, a quantum-inspired graph-pair Transformer that injects quantum-dynamical features from time series of node occupations and connected two-point correlators directly into the attention mechanism. We apply QDAGer to learning Graph Edit Distance (GED), an NP-hard similarity measure, using either a direct permutation-invariant embedding discrepancy or an alignment-based surrogate loss. Experiments on multiple GED benchmarks under different edit cost settings show that the proposed dynamical features provide a stronger inductive bias than classical structural alternatives under the same training protocol. In addition, we report ablations where the dynamical signal is replaced by standard random-walk and heat-kernel features while keeping the architecture fixed, highlighting that the gain comes from the injected dynamics rather than model capacity alone.

</details>

**Summary.** This work rigorously maps the boundaries in parameter space where a noisy Bell state retains specific quantum resources like entanglement or teleportation usefulness. By analyzing the singular-value flow of correlation tensors, the authors establish universal ordering chains between these operational thresholds. The findings are vital for understanding the robustness of quantum information protocols under realistic noise models.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides rigorous, quantitative boundaries for quantum resources in realistic noisy channels, which is crucial for designing fault-tolerant quantum communication protocols.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper characterizes how various operational quantum abilities (entanglement, teleportation usefulness, steerability, etc.) are maintained or lost in noisy Bell mixtures as the noise admixture changes.

**Main result.** The operational abilities are distinct, forming a universal ordered chain of thresholds as the noise parameter varies, and the unique separable/unsteerable point is found at zero noise admixture ($\lambda=0$).

**Method.** The analysis relies on characterizing the singular-value flow of the Pauli correlation tensor and using semidefinite programming to determine exact thresholds for various quantum witnesses.

**Model / system.** The system is a mixture of a pure Bell state ($\Phi^+$) and an arbitrary complex two-qubit X noise state ($\sigma_X$), parameterized by the Bell weight $\lambda$: $ho_\lambda = \lambda\Phi^+ + (1 - \lambda)\sigma_X$.

**Key observables.** Entanglement, teleportation usefulness, projective-measurement steerability, CJWR violation, and CHSH violation.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Bell weight ($\lambda$), relative phase ($	heta$) between noise coherence and Bell coherence, and the noise parameters defining $\sigma_X$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** For generic full-rank mixed X noise, upper bounds on steerability thresholds are obtained via finite-setting semidefinite programs.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates operational phase diagrams in the $(\lambda, 	heta)$ plane showing the nesting and widening of ability-absence intervals for various noise states.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically analyzes the thresholds for different operational abilities, deriving closed-form flows for specific noise types (like product noise) and proving the unique unsteerable point ($\lambda=0$) using recurrence relations for a swapping chain.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Noisy Bell mixtures may carry entanglement, standard teleportation usefulness, projective-measurement steerability, and optimized Cavalcanti-Jones-Wiseman-Reid (CJWR) and Clauser-Horne-Shimony-Holt (CHSH) violations. If the noise already has an ability, the mixture may lose and later recover it at distinct boundary crossings as the Bell weight decreases. We characterize ability-absence intervals and definitive thresholds for mixtures of $|Φ^+\rangle$ with arbitrary complex two-qubit $X$ noise. A closed-form singular-value flow of the Pauli correlation tensor gives the exact teleportation-useless, CJWR-satisfying, and CHSH-local intervals, while the positive partial transpose criterion gives the exact separable interval. At fixed populations and coherence magnitudes, these intervals widen as the relative phase between the $Φ$-block coherence of the noise and the Bell coherence increases from $0$ to $π$. The definitive thresholds form a universal chain from entanglement through teleportation usefulness and the three-setting CJWR witness to the common two-setting CJWR witness and CHSH-nonlocal threshold. Replacing teleportation usefulness by steerability gives a second chain, although their thresholds are not mutually ordered. For arbitrary pure two-qubit noise, the steerability thresholds in both directions for projective measurements and arbitrary positive operator-valued measures equal the entanglement threshold. For arbitrary product noise, an effective $X$-state singular-value flow gives the teleportation usefulness, CJWR witness, and CHSH-nonlocal thresholds. If a local noise factor is pure, the entanglement threshold and all four steerability thresholds are zero. For generic full-rank mixed $X$ noise, finite-setting semidefinite programs give upper bounds on the unknown directional projective-measurement steerability thresholds.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper analyzes the spread complexity of quantum states in large-N random matrix models by focusing on the Lanczos coefficients. It derives nonlinear equations governing these coefficients using the Coulomb-gas formalism. Crucially, it establishes a deep connection showing that these coefficients generate counts of planar maps, linking quantum dynamics to combinatorics.

**Why it may be interesting.** The connection between spectral properties (Lanczos coefficients) derived from complex many-body systems (RMT) and fundamental combinatorial objects (planar maps) provides a powerful, non-obvious mathematical structure applicable to understanding quantum chaos and complexity in physical systems.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates the physical meaning of Lanczos coefficients in the context of the spread complexity of the infinite-temperature thermofield double state within large-N random matrix theory.

**Main result.** The Lanczos coefficients are shown to satisfy nonlinear 'Lanczos equations' derived from the Coulomb-gas equation, and they generate counts of planar maps related to the geodesic distance.

**Method.** The analysis uses the orthogonal polynomial approach associated with the leading density of states, relating the dynamics to a three-term recurrence relation whose coefficients are the Lanczos coefficients.

**Model / system.** The study focuses on the infinite-temperature thermofield double state in large-N random matrix ensembles, which is analyzed via its associated orthogonal polynomials and the resulting Krylov dynamics.

**Key observables.** Spread complexity, Lanczos coefficients ($a_n, b_n$), and the leading density of states ($ho_0(E)$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Large-N limit, Krylov index, and the structure of the matrix potential (e.g., quartic potential).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on the large-N limit and the mapping of dynamics to the Krylov chain structure.

**Figures summary.** Not specified in the notes, but the text references figures illustrating the bijection between coefficients and planar maps.

**Paper structure.** The paper proceeds by establishing the connection between the dynamics (survival amplitude $S(t)$) and the orthogonal polynomial structure, deriving the recurrence relations, finding the nonlinear 'Lanczos equations' from the Coulomb-gas equation, and finally interpreting these coefficients combinatorially via planar maps.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We study the spread complexity of the infinite-temperature thermofield double state in large-$N$ random matrix theory and investigate the physical meaning of its Lanczos coefficients. The leading density of states defines a family of orthogonal polynomials whose recursion coefficients coincide with the Lanczos coefficients of the associated Krylov dynamics. Starting from the Coulomb-gas equation, we derive a pair of nonlinear relations for these coefficients, which we call the Lanczos equations. Since the Lanczos coefficients approach constants at large Krylov index, the spread complexity grows linearly at late times. We further show that the Lanczos coefficients can be interpreted as generating functions for planar maps. For an even potential, the difference $b_{n+1}^2-b_n^2$ generates maps with two legs whose marked legs are separated by the exact geodesic distance $n$. Consequently, the spread complexity acceleration operator equals twice the geodesic generating operator. We illustrate these results in the quartic matrix model, where the expansion in the quartic coupling counts tetravalent planar maps, and in the double-scaled Sachdev--Ye--Kitaev model, where the auxiliary Hilbert space can be identified with the physical chord Hilbert space.

</details>

**Summary.** This work addresses the challenge of designing high-capacity hard carbon anodes for sodium-ion batteries by creating a scalable computational workflow. It combines machine-learned potentials with structure generation to build a massive library of realistic carbon models. Crucially, it trains a neural network to predict the sodium storage capacity directly from the structure, allowing for rapid screening and the identification of optimal material designs.

**Why it may be interesting.** The application of machine learning (NNs) to predict macroscopic material properties (electrochemical capacity) from complex, disordered atomic structures is a prime example of ML-physics in action, relevant to understanding emergent behavior in disordered systems.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To develop a fundamental understanding and systematic design principles for high-capacity hard carbon anodes for sodium-ion batteries, overcoming the limitations of conventional atomistic methods in modeling complex, large-scale, disordered structures.

**Main result.** The developed framework successfully links hard carbon microstructure (density, porosity, pore morphology) to sodium-storage performance, identifying high-capacity candidates exceeding 800 mAh g-1.

**Method.** The study combines machine-learned interatomic potentials with a structure-generation framework, and then trains a lightweight neural-network surrogate to predict electrochemical capacity directly from the host structure.

**Model / system.** The system is hard carbon, a disordered carbon material used as an anode for sodium-ion batteries. The modeling involves generating large libraries of structures using MLIPs and predicting performance using a trained Neural Network (NN) surrogate model.

**Key observables.** Sodium storage capacity (mAh g-1), voltage profiles (V vs Na/Na+), carbon density, and $sp^3$ bonding fractions.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Porosity, carbon density (especially the threshold $\leq 2 	ext{ g cm}^{-3}$), and the $sp^3$ fraction are key structural descriptors.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The primary assumption is that the NN surrogate model can accurately predict the complex electrochemical performance based solely on structural descriptors, bypassing computationally prohibitive full intercalation calculations for the entire library.

**Figures summary.** Not specified, but the notes reference structural property summaries and validation plots comparing NN predictions to explicit calculations.

**Paper structure.** The paper follows a structure of (1) defining the physical problem and limitations of current methods, (2) proposing a combined MLIP/RAFFLE workflow for structure generation, (3) developing and training a NN surrogate model using structural and geometric descriptors, and (4) validating the model by predicting and identifying high-performance candidates.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Hard carbons are established anodes for lithium-ion batteries and leading candidates for sodium-ion batteries, yet their electrochemical performance is governed by a heterogeneous network of graphitic domains, defects, and nanopores that conventional atomistic methods cannot model at the required length scales. We combine universal machine-learned interatomic potentials with the RAFFLE structure-generation framework to construct 13,096 realistic hard carbon models containing up to 4,378 atoms, matching experimentally measured densities, porosities, and sp$^2$/sp$^3$ bonding fractions. Explicit sodium intercalation of representative structures reproduces the characteristic sloping-to-plateau voltage profiles, revealing that capacity increases with decreasing carbon density and increasing porosity. To screen the full library, we train a lightweight neural-network surrogate that predicts capacity directly from the host using frozen universal-potential descriptors augmented by geometric void features. The surrogate identifies high-capacity candidates exceeding 800 mAh g$^{-1}$, which are validated by full intercalation calculations. This scalable framework links hard carbon microstructure to sodium-storage performance and provides atomistic design principles for high-capacity anodes. More broadly, the workflow enables systematic exploration of synthesis-dependent amorphous microstructures, including precursor chemistry, pyrolysis, heteroatom doping, and pore engineering, providing a route toward atomistically informed hard carbon design.

</details>

**Summary.** This study systematically investigates how superconductivity in nickelate films is affected by external pressure and chemical substitution. By combining high-pressure/high-field transport measurements with DFT calculations, the authors establish a strong correlation between lattice structure, orbital energy levels, and the enhancement of the superconducting critical temperature.

**Why it may be interesting.** The work provides a detailed, multi-faceted approach linking macroscopic transport measurements (P, B) to microscopic electronic structure calculations (DFT, Wannier orbitals), which is highly relevant for understanding correlated electron behavior in novel superconducting materials.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study aims to bridge the understanding of high-temperature superconductivity in bilayer nickelates ($	ext{La}_2	ext{LnNi}_2	ext{O}_7$) between ambient and high-pressure regimes.

**Main result.** Superconductivity is enhanced under high pressure (up to 73 K at 16 GPa), and both ambient and high-pressure $T_c$ correlate with the evolution of normal-state transport behavior ($ho \propto T^a$) and structural changes.

**Method.** The research combines experimental measurements (resistivity under high magnetic fields and high pressure) with theoretical calculations using Density Functional Theory (DFT) to model the electronic structure.

**Model / system.** The system is the bilayer nickelate $	ext{La}_2	ext{LnNi}_2	ext{O}_7$ film. The electronic structure is modeled using a low-energy effective Hamiltonian derived from Wannier orbitals spanning the $	ext{Ni}3d$ and $	ext{O}2p$ bands, focusing on the interplay between $d_{x^2-y^2}$ and $d_{3z^2-r^2}$ orbitals.

**Key observables.** Superconducting critical temperature ($T_c$), normal-state resistivity exponent ($a$), lattice parameters ($c/a$ ratio), and orbital energy differences ($\Delta E$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** High pressures (up to 20 GPa), high magnetic fields (up to 59 T), and the lanthanide element ($	ext{Ln}$) substitution.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes that structural changes (pressure or substitution) directly modulate the electronic correlations governing superconductivity, as captured by the DFT model.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the structural dependence of $T_c$ on lattice constants, the evolution of the normal-state resistivity exponent ($a$) with pressure, and DFT results showing band structures and Fermi surfaces under different compression levels.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically investigates transport properties under extreme conditions (high P/B) and uses DFT to model the underlying electronic physics, correlating changes in orbital character and lattice structure to the superconducting transition temperature.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

The discovery of high critical-temperature $T_{\mathrm{c}}$ superconductivity near 80 K in bilayer nickelates under high pressure has sparked extensive studies. While superconductivity exceeding 40 K was subsequently discovered at ambient pressure in compressively strained films, the relationship between ambient- and high-pressure regimes remains an open question. Here we present a systematic investigation of superconductivity in compressively strained La$_2$LnNi$_2$O$_7$ films (Ln = lanthanides) at ambient and high pressures. The normal-state resistivity at ambient pressure, revealed by suppressing superconductivity with magnetic fields of 59 T, tends toward $T^2$ behaviour. Under high pressure in a cubic-anvil cell, $T_{\mathrm{c}}$ was enhanced from 41-42 K at ambient pressure to 67-73 K at 16 GPa. On the other hand, lattice compression induced by Ln substitution, which may mimic effects of pressure, lowers $T_{\mathrm{c}}$. In both cases, $T_{\mathrm{c}}$ correlates with the evolution of normal-state transport between $T^2$ and $T$-linear behaviour, offering insight into the interplay between lattice structure and superconductivity in bilayer nickelates.

</details>

### [Entanglement assisted quantum $(r,δ)$-locally recoverable codes](http://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17118v1)

**Authors:** Carlos Galindo, Fernando Hernando, Helena Martín-Cruz, Ryutaroh Matsumoto  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** quantum information and computing · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2608.17118v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks

<details open><summary>📷 Fig 1</summary>

<img src="2608.17118_figures/2608.17118_fig1.jpg" width="500"><br>
<sub>Figure 1. Sets ∆a,b and ∆σ a,b in Proposition 4.1</sub>

</details>

**Summary.** This paper introduces the framework for entanglement-assisted quantum locally recoverable codes, generalizing existing quantum error-correcting codes. It derives necessary and sufficient conditions for these codes and constructs optimal examples using established classical code families. This advances the theoretical understanding of quantum information protection against localized erasures.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides advanced theoretical tools for quantum information security, directly relevant to designing robust quantum communication channels against localized noise.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper aims to develop and characterize entanglement-assisted quantum $(r, \delta)$-locally recoverable codes (EA $(r, \delta)$-QLRCs).

**Main result.** The authors establish necessary and sufficient conditions for these codes and construct optimal pure EA $(r, \delta)$-QLRCs using known classical code families like BCH and J-affine variety codes.

**Method.** The methodology involves establishing theoretical bounds, deriving necessary and sufficient conditions based on algebraic structures (Euclidean/Hermitian inner products), and providing explicit constructions parameterized by classical code properties.

**Model / system.** The model is Quantum Error-Correcting Codes (QECCs) designed to protect quantum states from local erasures, generalized by incorporating entanglement assistance via receiver-held qudits.

**Key observables.** Code parameters like $[[n, k, d; c]]_q$, and the locality parameters $(r, \delta)$ defining the error correction capability.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The locality parameters $(r, \delta)$, the number of entangled pairs ($c$), and the underlying finite field structure ($\mathbb{F}_q$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The local recovery operation is assumed to be assisted by receiver-held qudits that remain unaffected by erasures.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds from background material on classical and quantum LRCs, formalizes the EA $(r, \delta)$-QLRC framework, derives necessary and sufficient conditions, and concludes with explicit constructions of optimal codes from various classical code families.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Quantum $(r,δ)$-locally recoverable codes are quantum error-correcting codes capable of correcting $δ-1$ qudit erasures within one subset of qudits of cardinality at most $r+δ-1$. In this paper, we introduce the more general framework of entanglement-assisted quantum $(r,δ)$-locally recoverable codes, assuming that the local recovery operation is assisted by receiver-held qudits that remain unaffected by erasures.   We establish necessary and sufficient conditions for these codes to satisfy this property. For codes derived from Hermitian or Euclidean constructions, we establish connections between entanglement-assisted quantum and classical notions of $(r,δ)$-local recoverability, and derive a Singleton-like bound. Furthermore, we construct optimal pure entan\-gle\-ment-assisted quantum $(r,δ)$-locally recoverable codes from several families of classical codes, including bivariate $J$-affine variety codes, BCH codes, and homothetic-BCH codes.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper rigorously proves a positive-order violation of Rényi additivity for a known pair of quantum channels. By developing two independent, computationally verifiable methods, the authors establish a certified interval ($0 < p \le 1/22$) for this violation. This work significantly advances the theoretical understanding of quantum channel properties by providing a concrete, checkable mathematical certificate.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a highly rigorous, computationally verifiable certificate for a fundamental quantum information phenomenon (Rényi additivity violation), which is crucial for understanding the limits of quantum channel capacity.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To provide the first printed, computer-verifiable certified positive-order endpoint for the violation of Rényi additivity for a specific pair of quantum channels.

**Main result.** The authors prove a strict violation of Rényi additivity, $S_p^{\min}(N_R\otimes N_{ar S}) < S_p^{\min}(N_R)+S_p^{\min}(N_{ar S})$, for the interval $0 < p \le 1/22$.

**Method.** The proof uses elementary, certificate-based methods, including staircase arguments and derivative analysis, relying on comparisons of integers to establish the required inequality.

**Model / system.** The study concerns two explicit quantum channels, $N_R$ and $ar{N}_S$, acting on specific subspaces derived from the CHLMW example.

**Key observables.** Minimum output Rényi entropy, $S_{\min}^p(N) = \min_{ho} S_p(N(ho))$, and the additivity inequality.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The violation is certified for the order $0 < p \le 1/22$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The proof relies on establishing the inequality $A(p)^2 > B(p)$ for the specified interval, and the endpoint $1/22$ is not claimed to be optimal.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the problem by referencing previous work, then uses eigenvalue bounds and concavity arguments to set up the core inequality, which is finally proven using two independent, rigorous methods (staircase and derivative) to certify the interval $(0, 1/22]$.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Cubitt, Harrow, Leung, Montanaro, and Winter (CHLMW) exhibited an explicit pair of quantum channels whose minimum output Rényi entropy is nonadditive at order zero, and reported numerical violations at positive orders close to zero. Their paper states that a semidefinite-programming argument yields a rigorous positive-order interval for the same example, without printing the endpoint or a verifiable certificate; a recent paper by Leung, Lovitz, and Wu records that for this pair "no rigorous endpoint was obtained". We close that gap for the trace-preserving normalization of the printed pair fixed in Section 2. Small rational witness matrices prove that every output of either channel has all eigenvalues between $301/100000$ and $2/3$; a single explicit entangled input has an exact rational joint output spectrum of rank eight; and two independent elementary interval arguments turn these three facts into a proof of strict additivity violation, $S_p^{\min}(\mathrm{N}_R\otimes\mathrm{N}_{\bar S}) < S_p^{\min}(\mathrm{N}_R)+S_p^{\min}(\mathrm{N}_{\bar S}),$ for every real order $0<p\le 1/22$. Every step of the verification reduces to comparisons of integers, and the complete certificate is a few small rational matrices that a reader can check with a short program -- or, for any single order, by hand. To our knowledge, consistent with the assessment of Leung, Lovitz, and Wu, this is the first printed, computer-verifiable certified positive-order endpoint for this explicit pair. We claim no novelty for the phenomenon or for the eigenvalue-floor mechanism, both due to CHLMW, and no optimality of the endpoint.

</details>
