**Summary.** This paper rigorously solves for the optimal fixed-particle-number constant in the Riesz Lieb--Oxford inequality. By establishing strict monotonicity ($\Lambda_{N+1} > \Lambda_N$) through advanced techniques involving concentration-compactness, it proves that the optimal constant is never achieved at any finite particle number. This provides a deep mathematical understanding of the scaling behavior of quantum many-body energy bounds.

**Why it may be interesting.** The rigorous mathematical treatment of optimal constants in many-body quantum systems, particularly those involving interaction potentials like the Riesz potential, is crucial for understanding ground state properties in quantum matter.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper determines the optimal fixed-particle-number constant $\Lambda_N(s, d)$ in the Riesz Lieb--Oxford inequality.

**Main result.** It proves that $\Lambda_N$ is attained for all $N \ge 1$ and establishes strict monotonicity: $\Lambda_1 < \Lambda_2 < \Lambda_3 < \cdots$.

**Method.** The proof combines grand-canonical concentration--compactness techniques with a strict one-particle extension, forming an inductive argument.

**Model / system.** The analysis concerns symmetric probability measures $P$ on $(\mathbb{R}^d)^N$, relating the indirect Riesz energy $	ext{Eind}[P]$ to the multimarginal optimal-transport cost $W_N(ho)$.

**Key observables.** The optimal constant $\Lambda_N(s, d)$, the one-body density $ho$, and the associated energy functionals $G_N(ho)$ and $L(ho)$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Spatial dimension $d \ge 1$, interaction exponent $0 < s < d$, and the fixed particle number $N \ge 1$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on established results like the compact-support theorem of Di Marino and Lelotte, and the ability to analyze maximizing sequences via recentering.

**Paper structure.** The structure follows an inductive proof: first establishing attainment for fixed $N$, then using the compact-support theorem to prove the strict inequality $\Lambda_{N+1} > \Lambda_N$, thereby closing the induction from $N=1$.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Let $\mathsf{d}\geq1$, $0<\mathsf{s}<\mathsf{d}$, and $N\geq1$. We prove that the optimal fixed-particle-number constant $Λ_N(\mathsf{s},\mathsf{d})$ in the Riesz Lieb--Oxford inequality is attained and that these constants are strictly increasing in $N$. The proof combines grand-canonical concentration--compactness with a strict one-particle extension. After recentering, a limiting plan arising from a maximizing sequence may assign positive probability to several particle numbers and hence be grand-canonical. A strict $N$-particle completion excludes this case, while the inequalities $Λ_N>Λ_k$ for $k<N$ exclude limits with a fixed lower particle number. Once attainment at particle number $N$ is known, the compact-support theorem of Di Marino and Lelotte arXiv:2607.11440, valid for all $0<\mathsf{s}<\mathsf{d}$, permits a non-product one-particle extension and yields $Λ_{N+1}(\mathsf{s},\mathsf{d})>Λ_N(\mathsf{s},\mathsf{d})$. Together, these implications close an induction beginning at $N=1$.

</details>

### [Gaussian Optimality of Energy-Constrained One-Shot Communication through Single-Mode Bosonic Gaussian Channels](http://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17239v1)

**Authors:** Se-Wan Ji  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** quantum information and computing · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2608.17239v1>

**Summary.** This paper rigorously proves that the one-shot Holevo capacity for single-mode bosonic Gaussian channels, subject to an energy constraint, is achieved by Gaussian states. By employing advanced optimization techniques and continuity arguments, the authors establish exact formulas for the capacity across all operational regimes. This result is vital for understanding the fundamental limits of quantum information transmission through linear bosonic channels.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides fundamental, exact capacity formulas for continuous-variable quantum channels, which are crucial for designing high-rate quantum communication protocols in quantum optics.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper aims to prove Gaussian optimality for the energy-constrained one-shot Holevo capacity of single-mode bosonic Gaussian channels.

**Main result.** Gaussian optimality is proven for the energy-constrained one-shot Holevo capacity for all single-mode Gaussian channels, meaning the capacity is determined by Gaussian optimization techniques.

**Method.** The proof utilizes Gaussian optimization theorems, the Matsumoto–Shimono–Winter identity, and continuity arguments across different channel regimes (low-energy vs. high-energy).

**Model / system.** The system involves single-mode bosonic Gaussian channels, which model linear quantum communication links (e.g., optical or microwave). The analysis uses covariance matrices and Gaussian states.

**Key observables.** Holevo capacity ($\chi$), Entanglement of Formation ($EF$), and the energy constraint ($	ext{Tr} ho \hat{N} \le ar{N}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The analysis distinguishes between low-energy and high-energy (water-filling) regimes, and the result is explicitly for one-shot communication.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The result is strictly for one-shot communication and does not assume additivity across multiple channel uses.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the exact unrestricted one-shot capacity for a phase-sensitive attenuator, showing the low-energy branch joining the water-filling regime at a specific threshold.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds the proof by establishing bounds ($	ext{disp} \le \chi_G \le \chi$) and then using continuity arguments across the parameter space of single-mode Gaussian channels to show the equality of these bounds.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We prove Gaussian optimality for the energy-constrained one-shot Holevo capacity of single-mode bosonic Gaussian channels, including phase-sensitive channels with arbitrarily squeezed thermal noise. The unresolved regime is the low energy branch, where the Gaussian optimizer modulates only one quadrature and minimum-output-entropy arguments cannot decouple the average state from the letters. For pure one-mode dilations we retain a stronger pointwise result: moment-matched Gaussianization improves the fixed average Holevo function for every input. For mixed environments, whose purification produces a 1:2 entanglement-of-formation problem, we avoid any generic 1:2 Gaussian extremality conjecture. The optimal Gaussian letter selects an effective environmental Schmidt mode and an affine two-mode EPR witness. Its null direction is exactly the modulated quadrature, while its slope equals the negative derivative of the Gaussian letter-output entropy. This produces a supporting lower bound on the dilated entanglement of formation; Gaussian maximum entropy and concavity then give a global upper bound tangent at the Gaussian optimizer. Consequently the known Gaussian formulas for attenuating, amplifying, phase-conjugating, and additive-noise fiducial channels are exact over unrestricted ensembles at every input energy. Via the passive-input fiducial decomposition and energy-constrained continuity, the result extends to every single-mode Gaussian channel, including lower-rank canonical limits. No additivity across channel uses is assumed.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper analyzes the computational difficulty of decoding errors in topological quantum codes like the surface and color codes. By establishing strong inapproximability results based on 3SAT hardness, it proves that finding the minimum-weight error is computationally intractable for large systems. This work sets theoretical benchmarks for the required efficiency of quantum error correction decoding algorithms.

**Why it may be interesting.** The hardness results provide fundamental theoretical limits on the efficiency of quantum error correction decoding algorithms, which is crucial for designing practical fault-tolerant quantum computers.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper studies the computational complexity of minimum-weight decoding for various two-dimensional topological quantum codes.

**Main result.** Assuming P!=NP, the authors establish polynomial additive inapproximability gaps for decoding problems on surface codes and color codes, meaning these problems are hard to approximate efficiently.

**Method.** The core method involves developing a general framework to embed logical constraints into coupled primal-dual join problems on a lattice, using reductions from 3SAT and Håstad's hardness of approximation.

**Model / system.** The study focuses on topological quantum codes, specifically the surface code (planar and toric) and the 4.8.8 color code, which are used to protect quantum information.

**Key observables.** The minimum weight of the Pauli error consistent with syndrome measurements, and the resulting inapproximability gaps ($\Omega(N^{1/14})$ or $\Omega(N^{1/18})$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** N (the number of qubits), and the lattice structure parameters ($\Delta, \delta$) used in the geometric analysis of path weights.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The results are conditional on the assumption that P != NP.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the geometric structures used in the proof, showing connections, double connections, and the regions (red/blue boxes) where primal and dual paths interact during the weight comparison analysis.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes NP-hardness via reductions from 3SAT, then develops a modular framework for the Minimum-Weight Join problem, and finally applies this framework to derive specific inapproximability bounds for the toric, planar, and 4.8.8 codes.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Efficient decoding is essential for the practical realization of fault-tolerant quantum computers. We study the computational complexity of minimum-weight decoding for topological quantum codes. For surface codes under the depolarizing channel, we consider Minimum-Weight decoding, which seeks a minimum-weight Pauli error consistent with both the $X$- and $Z$-syndromes. For color codes under independent $X$- and $Z$-error models, we consider Separate Minimum-Weight decoding.   Assuming $P\neq NP$, we establish polynomial additive inapproximability gaps for these problems. Specifically, for the toric code and the $4.8.8$ color code on the torus, no polynomial-time algorithm can always produce a solution whose weight is within $Ω(N^{1/14})$ of the optimum, where $N$ is the number of qubits. For the planar surface code, we obtain an $Ω(N^{1/18})$ gap. Our inapproximability results use Håstad's hardness of approximation for MAX-3SAT.   Our reduction develops a general, modular framework for embedding logical constraints into coupled primal--dual join problems on a lattice. A key ingredient is a localization argument that controls unintended interactions between different parts of the construction.

</details>

**Summary.** This work tackles the difficult ML detection problem in high-dimensional M-QAM MIMO systems by formulating it as an Ising spin-glass model. The authors introduce a novel, size-scalable approach by using the ML rate—the exponential rate of the optimal solution—as the objective function to design QAOA parameters. This method shows superior performance compared to traditional energy-based quantum approaches.

**Why it may be interesting.** The connection between quantum optimization algorithms (QAOA) and complex physical detection problems (MIMO) via spin-glass theory is highly relevant. The use of the ML rate as a design parameter bridges quantum information theory with advanced signal processing.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the NP-hard problem of Maximum-Likelihood (ML) detection in M-QAM Multiple-Input Multiple-Output (MIMO) systems.

**Main result.** The authors develop a correlated infinite-size multi-species spin-glass framework and show that using the ML rate for QAOA angle design yields significant performance gains over energy-based designs.

**Method.** The methodology involves mapping the detection problem to an Ising cost function, developing a correlated spin-glass surrogate, and utilizing the ML rate as a novel, size-scalable objective for QAOA angle optimization.

**Model / system.** The system is an M-QAM MIMO channel, modeled by a cost function derived from minimizing the squared Euclidean distance between received and transmitted signals. This is mapped onto an Ising Hamiltonian structure, which is analyzed using correlated infinite-size spin-glass theory.

**Key observables.** ML Rate (exponential rate of sampling the ML string), Binary Error Rate (BER), and the energy density $\langlear{C}_qangle/q$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** M-QAM constellation size (up to 4096-QAM), number of antennas (up to 128), and Signal-to-Noise Ratio (SNR) up to 45 dB.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The framework develops a correlated spin-glass surrogate, which is not mathematically proven to equal the true physical energy density limit, and the analysis relies on specific scaling assumptions for the infinite-size limit.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare performance metrics like SER across various detection methods (exact ML, offline design, WS pipelines) for different QAM constellations, demonstrating the advantage of the sampling-designed angles.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the ML detection problem and its mapping to an Ising model. It then develops the correlated infinite-size spin-glass framework, proposing the ML rate as a novel angle design objective. Finally, it presents simulation results showing the superior performance of the ML-rate-guided QAOA approach.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

The quantum approximate optimization algorithm (QAOA) targets NP-hard maximum-likelihood (ML) detection in multiple-input multiple-output (MIMO) systems. Existing $M$-ary quadrature amplitude modulation (M-QAM) detectors design angles by expected Ising energy: online per instance, warm-started, or ramped, while train-once designs remain B/QPSK-only or block-local, leaving M-QAM without a size-scalable benchmark. Their infinite-size spin-glass theory assumes independent disorder, matching the retained covariances at B/QPSK but not M-QAM's correlated couplings and fields. We develop a correlated infinite-size multi-species spin-glass framework whose covariance-matched evaluators make that energy an offline objective with a size-scalable benchmark. In addition, the ML rate, the exponential rate of sampling the ML string, is for the first time exploited for QAOA angle design in MIMO detection. The energy evaluator is $q$-free at $O(p\,4^p)$ cost while the ML rate transfers angles from a fixed $q_{\rm ref}$-qubit reference. Tests reach 4096-QAM, 128 antennas, $p=30$ and per-symbol SNR 0-45 dB. In simulations, ML rates fall as a power law $r_0\,p^{-α}$, with larger exponents for the sampling design, which tracks exact ML at $5\times5$ 16-QAM (0-20 dB) and $3\times3$ 64-QAM (8-28 dB) while its bit-error rate (BER) advantage widens with SNR to two orders of magnitude. The approach points toward near-optimum decoding on deeper noiseless fault-tolerant quantum (FTQ) circuits.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper rigorously analyzes the decay rate of the probability that a symmetric $\alpha$-stable AR(1) sequence remains positive over time as the autoregressive coefficient approaches one. It establishes that this persistence rate scales logarithmically with the inverse of the coefficient, providing a precise constant $G^*_\alpha$ that characterizes this near-unit-root transition.

**Why it may be interesting.** The study of persistence rates in heavy-tailed, long-range dependent processes is crucial for understanding the stability and memory effects in complex physical systems, including those modeled by fractional dynamics or anomalous diffusion.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper studies the persistence probability (survival probability) of an AR(1) sequence driven by symmetric $\alpha$-stable innovations as the autoregressive coefficient approaches unity (near-unit-root behavior).

**Main result.** The persistence rate $\Lambda(a,\alpha)$ scales as $\log(1/a)$ as $a \uparrow 1$, and the ratio $\Lambda(a,\alpha)/\log(1/a)$ converges to a limit $G^*_\alpha \in (0, \alpha/2]$.

**Method.** The analysis involves relating the discrete-time AR(1) process to a continuous stable Lévy process via geometric time embedding and using techniques like subadditivity and coupling arguments to establish tight bounds.

**Model / system.** The model is an AR(1) recursion: $X_n = aX_{n-1} + \xi_n$, where $\xi_n$ are i.i.d. symmetric $\alpha$-stable random variables ($0 < \alpha \le 2$). This process is shown to be related to a stable Ornstein-Uhlenbeck process.

**Key observables.** Persistence probability $Q_n = P(X_1 > 0, \dots, X_n > 0)$ and its exponential rate $\Lambda(a,\alpha) = -\lim_{n	o\infty} rac{1}{n} \log Q_n$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Autoregressive coefficient $a \in (0, 1)$, Stability index $\alpha \in (0, 2]$, and the limit $a \uparrow 1$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on the exact representation of the AR(1) chain as a sampled stable Lévy process, and the comparison between discrete and continuous survival probabilities.

**Figures summary.** Not specified in the notes.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the existence of the exponential rate $\Lambda(a,\alpha)$ by relating the discrete AR(1) process to a continuous stable Lévy process. It derives upper and lower bounds for $\Lambda(a,\alpha)$ and finally determines the asymptotic behavior as $a 	o 1$ by connecting it to dense-sampling limits for the stable Ornstein-Uhlenbeck process.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Persistence changes character as an autoregressive coefficient approaches one: for each fixed $0 < a < 1$, survival above zero decays exponentially, whereas at the unit root symmetric random-walk survival is of order $n^{-1/2}$. We study this transition for AR($1$) sequences driven by symmetric $α$-stable innovations and write $Λ(a,α)$ for their exponential persistence rate. The entire chain admits an exact representation through a single stable Lévy process observed on a geometrically expanding time grid. Comparison with continuous half-line survival gives $Λ(a,α) \leq \fracα{2}\log{(1/a)}$. For $0 < α< 2$, this bound disproves the stable specialization of a conjecture of Hinrichs, Kolb and Wachtel for regularly varying innovation tails. To obtain a lower bound of the same near-unit order, we combine stable closure under subsampling with a monotonicity coupling. This proves $Λ(a,α) \asymp \log{(1/a)}$ as $a \uparrow 1$ and shows that the ratio $Λ(a,α)/\log{(1/a)}$ converges to a limit in $(0,α/2]$, equal to its supremum over $0 < a < 1$. Finally, a Lamperti transformation reduces identification of this constant to a dense-sampling persistence problem for a stationary stable Ornstein--Uhlenbeck process. Existing Gaussian theory determines the sharp value at $α=2$. For $0 < α< 2$, identifying the value requires controlling paths that cross below zero and return above zero between consecutive observations.

</details>

**Authors:** Muhammad Ahsan Shakeel, Saad Muzammil, Danyal Tayyub, Muhammad Faryad  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** quantum information and computing · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2608.17495v1>

(b) Bit-Flip: Entangled architectures (ZZ, Pauli) degrade sharply at p ≥0.05; Amplitude holds 100% through p = 0.10. (c) Phase-Flip: Z-encoding (yellow) is perfectly flat at 80% for all error rates, confirming Phase-Flip immunity via [RZ(θ), Z] = 0. (b) Training accuracy: Variants with poor test accuracy simultaneously maintain near-100% training accuracy, confirming noise-induced overfitting. Amplitude (top row) exceeds 0.91 for all channels; ZZ (bottom row) drops to 0.762 under Bit-Flip and Phase-Flip, the clearest evidence of entanglement-mediated noise vulnerability in this...</sub>

**Summary.** This work investigates the robustness of Quantum Support Vector Machines (QSVMs) against noise on NISQ devices by comparing various quantum feature map encodings. The authors find that amplitude-inspired feature maps are significantly more resilient than entangled or simple maps. This provides essential guidelines for practitioners on selecting appropriate encoding schemes for near-term quantum machine learning applications.

**Why it may be interesting.** The findings provide concrete, data-driven criteria for selecting quantum feature maps suitable for near-term quantum hardware, which is crucial for developing practical quantum machine learning algorithms.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper assesses how different feature map encodings affect the noise resilience of Quantum Support Vector Machines (QSVMs) when implemented on Noisy Intermediate-Scale Quantum (NISQ) hardware.

**Main result.** Amplitude-inspired feature maps proved significantly more robust than entangled or simple feature maps, maintaining high accuracy up to moderate noise levels ($p=0.10$).

**Method.** The authors simulated QSVM performance by encoding classical data into quantum states using four feature maps (Z, ZZ, Pauli, Amplitude-Inspired) and testing them under three noise channels (Depolarizing, Bit-Flip, Phase-Flip).

**Model / system.** The study uses QSVM trained on kernel matrices derived from quantum circuits. The noise is modeled using standard Pauli error channels applied to the quantum state.

**Key observables.** Test and training classification accuracy, generalization gap (Train - Test accuracy), and noise tolerance (maximum error probability $p$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Noise probabilities $p \in \{0.01, 0.05, 0.10, 0.50\}$; Noise channels: Depolarizing, Bit-Flip, Phase-Flip.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The noise modeling assumes independent errors on each wire for two-qubit gates; the results are based on simulation and require validation on physical hardware.

**Figures summary.** Figures trace accuracy vs. noise probability ($p$) for various feature maps under different noise channels, while tables summarize the performance across the 52 total experimental runs.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically compares four feature map architectures (Z, ZZ, Pauli, Amplitude-Inspired) by testing them across a grid of noise levels and noise types, culminating in data-driven guidelines for feature map selection.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Gate-level noise degrades the classification accuracy of Quantum Support Vector Machines (QSVMs) on Noisy Intermediate-Scale Quantum (NISQ) hardware, and the degree of degradation depends on how classical data is encoded into quantum states. We tested Z, ZZ, a Pauli, and an amplitude-inspired feature maps under depolarizing, bit-flip, and phase-flip noise channels in $52$ controlled experiments with error probabilities $p =0.01, 0.05, 0.10$, and $0.50$. The amplitude-inspired feature map had $100$\% test accuracy up to $p = 0.10$ across all three noise channels, while other feature maps fell to $65$-$90$\% under the same noise level and type. The Z feature map was found to be immune to phase-flip noise to a significantly high error rate, a consequence of the commutation relation $[R_Z, Z] = 0$. Entangled circuit variants produced generalization gaps in train-test sets of up to $17.5$\% under noise, whereas the amplitude variants maintained zero gap throughout. These results give practitioners data-driven criteria for a feature map on near-term quantum hardware.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper presents an improved method for compiling general unitary quantum circuits into the resource-constrained Clifford+T model. By employing block flattening and Quantum Singular Value Transformation, the authors reduce the worst-case T-gate count scaling from $O(2^{4n/3})$ to $O(2^{5n/4})$. This advancement is crucial for making large-scale, fault-tolerant quantum computation more resource-efficient.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses the resource overhead in fault-tolerant quantum computation, which is critical for realizing large-scale quantum processors.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The central problem is synthesizing arbitrary n-qubit unitaries using the minimum possible number of non-Clifford gates (T-count) for fault-tolerant quantum compilation.

**Main result.** The authors achieve a worst-case T-count scaling of O(2^(5n/4)), improving upon previous bounds of O(2^(4n/3)).

**Method.** The technique involves treating the target unitary as a block-encoded object, which is then flattened using block flattening and recovered via Quantum Singular Value Transformation (QSVT).

**Model / system.** The model is quantum computation implemented by unitary transformations U in the Clifford+T circuit model, acting on n qubits.

**Key observables.** T-count (the primary cost measure), approximation error (ε), and the exponential scaling of the T-count.

**Important parameters / regimes.** n (number of qubits), ε (approximation error), and the scaling exponents (e.g., 5/4).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes that the error $\log(1/\epsilon)$ is polynomial in $n$, and that classical pre-processing time is excluded from the quantum resource count.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first establishing the need for efficient unitary synthesis, then detailing the block flattening technique using Walsh transforms, followed by the block encoding via Julia-Halmos dilation, and finally applying QSVT to achieve the improved T-count bounds.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Synthesizing arbitrary $n$-qubit unitaries using as few non-Clifford gates as possible is a central problem in fault-tolerant quantum compilation. We present a Clifford+$T$ quantum circuit construction that approximately implements any classically specified unitary to within error $ε$ and achieves a worst-case $T$-count with leading exponential scaling of $2^{5n/4}$ whenever $\log(1/ε)=\operatorname{poly}(n)$. This improves upon the best previous $2^{4n/3}$ scaling. The key innovation lies in treating the target unitary as a single block-encoded object rather than a long product of simpler operations. A technique of block flattening controls the normalization while preserving an efficient implementation of the block encoding; subsequently, quantum singular value transformation maps its common singular value to one, thereby recovering the target unitary.

</details>

### [Simplicial closure fragments the explosive cooperation transitions in higher-order public goods games](http://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17968v1)

**Authors:** Wenjia Rao, Jianneng Huang  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** disordered systems and neural networks · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2608.17968v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks

<details open><summary>📷 Fig 1</summary>

<img src="2608.17968_figures/2608.17968_fig1.jpg" width="500"><br>
<sub>FIG. 1. Final cooperation ratio ρC as a function of the multiplication factor r in (a) randomized hypergraph (HG) and (b) simplicial complex (SC). Results are shown for two payoff exponents, γ = 0.5 and γ = 2, and two strengths of the three-body interaction, α = 0.1 and α = 0.2. In the HG, the concave case γ = 0.5 exhibits a gradual continuous-like transition, whereas the convex case γ = 2 leads to a much sharper, discontinuous-like transition. In the SC, the concave regime remains qualitatively similar, but the convex regime becomes much smoother. For visual clarity, a moving average with a window of three adjacent r values is applied to better display the shapes of the transition curves.</sub>

</details>

<details><summary>📷 Fig 2</summary>

<img src="2608.17968_figures/2608.17968_fig2.jpg" width="500"><br>
<sub>FIG. 2. Distributions of final cooperation ratio ρC near the transition region for (a) γ = 0.5 and (b) γ = 2. For γ = 0.5, both representations produce single-peaked distributions centered at intermediate cooperation levels, although the SC distribution is broader. For γ = 2, the HG exhibits a strongly bimodal distribution, indicating global bistability. In contrast, the SC produces a broad distribution over intermediate cooperation levels, showing that simplicial closure fragments the global bistability of the HG into a broad ensemble of metastable final states.</sub>

</details>

<details><summary>📷 Fig 3</summary>

<img src="2608.17968_figures/2608.17968_fig3.jpg" width="500"><br>
<sub>FIG. 3. Hysteresis scans for the convex payoff regime γ = 2 in (a) HG and (b) SC. Solid lines denote scans with increasing r, and dashed lines denote scans with decreasing r. The HG displays a pronounced hysteresis loop, consistent with a first-order-like transition and global bistability. In the SC, the hysteresis loop is strongly reduced but does not disappear, indicating that simplicial closure does not simply turn the explosive transition into an ordinary continuous transition. Instead, it produces a smeared discontinuous-like regime with residual path dependence.</sub>

</details>

<details><summary>📷 Fig 4</summary>

<img src="2608.17968_figures/2608.17968_fig4.jpg" width="500"><br>
<sub>FIG. 4. Distributions of the final-state configuration overlap Q for (a) γ = 0.5 and (b) γ = 2. The overlap is computed from randomly sampled pairs of independently evolved final strategy configurations. For γ = 0.5, both HG and SC exhibit single-peaked overlap distributions centered at intermediate values; the SC distribution is broader but remains regular and bell- shaped. For γ = 2, the HG shows strong weight near both low and high overlap values, consistent with a compact two-basin structure. In contrast, the SC distribution becomes broad and flatter over intermediate overlaps, indicating a distributed set of distinct metastable final configurations induced by simplicial closure.</sub>

</details>

<details><summary>📷 Fig 5</summary>

<img src="2608.17968_figures/2608.17968_fig5.jpg" width="500"><br>
<sub>FIG. 5. Time-resolved probability-density map of the normalized largest cooperative component SCC max(t)/N in the convex regime γ = 2. Panel (a) shows the HG, and panel (b) shows the SC. In the HG, trajectories rapidly separate into two dominant branches: collapse of cooperative clusters or growth into a system-spanning cooperative component. In the SC, substantial probability remains over a wide range of intermediate component sizes, showing that cooperative nuclei can persist locally for long times without necessarily developing into a global cascade.</sub>

</details>

**Summary.** This work compares cooperation dynamics in higher-order public goods games modeled on hypergraphs (HG) versus simplicial complexes (SC). The key finding is that simplicial closure selectively restructures the system's attractor landscape: it fragments sharp, first-order cooperation transitions into a broad ensemble of metastable states. This demonstrates that the geometric representation of interactions fundamentally dictates the nature of collective phase transitions.

**Why it may be interesting.** The concept of structural constraints (like simplicial closure) leading to qualitative changes in collective dynamics, particularly the fragmentation of stable attractors, is relevant to understanding phase transitions in complex systems modeled by network theory.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates how the structural constraint of simplicial closure (SC) alters the cooperative dynamics observed in higher-order public goods games (PGGs) compared to the more general hypergraph (HG) model.

**Main result.** Simplicial closure selectively fragments the compact bistability seen in explosive, first-order cooperation transitions, replacing it with a broad ensemble of metastable states, while having only quantitative effects on continuous transitions.

**Method.** The authors compare the mean final cooperation level and the distribution of final states by simulating PGGs on both HG and SC representations derived from the same backbone.

**Model / system.** The model is a higher-order Public Goods Game (PGG) incorporating pairwise and three-player interactions, analyzed using a Fermi imitation rule for strategy updating.

**Key observables.** Mean final cooperation level ($\rho_C$), the shape of the cooperation transition (continuous vs. first-order), and the distribution of final-state configuration overlaps ($P(Q)$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The payoff exponent $\gamma$ (controlling payoff curvature, e.g., $\gamma=0.5$ vs. $\gamma=2$) and the degree of closure interpolation ($p$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The comparison assumes that the only difference between HG and SC is the enforcement of simplicial closure on the three-player interactions.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate that for first-order transitions ($\gamma=2$), HG shows compact bimodal bistability, whereas SC shows a broad distribution of metastable states, and this effect is visible across different payoff forms.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the model, compares the HG and SC representations across different payoff regimes ($\gamma$), and systematically analyzes the resulting changes in transition shape, bistability structure, and final-state distributions.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Hypergraph (HG) and simplicial complex (SC) are two common representations of higher-order networks, and are often expected to differ mainly quantitatively when they encode comparable group interactions. Here we show that this expectation fails in evolutionary cooperation dynamics. Using a controlled higher-order public goods game (PGG) with minimal ad hoc parameters, we compare cooperation transitions on randomized HG and SC constructed from the same triangular backbone. We find that the impact of simplicial closure is selective: when the cooperation transition is continuous-like on HG, imposing simplicial closure mainly broadens the transition without changing its qualitative nature; however, when the transition is explosive and first-order-like on HG, simplicial closure will fragment the compact low/high bistability into a broad ensemble of metastable final states. Detailed analysis reveals that this selective effect arises from the dual role of simplicial closure in cooperative-nucleus dynamics: it promotes the survival of local cooperative nuclei while suppressing their conversion into system-wide cascades. Further experiments confirm that this fragmentation is not tied to a specific payoff form, but is a generic feature of explosive cooperation transitions in higher-order PGGs.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical study treats the Bethe-Heitler process as a quantum laboratory to study multipartite entanglement among the final electron, proton, and photon. By analyzing the helicity structure and using entanglement measures, the authors predict and simulate the existence of numerous GHZ and W states. The results confirm that genuine tripartite entanglement can be generated in this process, although these states occupy a much smaller volume in phase space than simpler bipartite entangled states.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work applies quantum information concepts (GHZ/W states) to a well-understood, high-energy particle physics process, providing a concrete, calculable physical realization for testing entanglement generation mechanisms.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study focuses on quantifying and characterizing multipartite entanglement, specifically GHZ and W states, generated during the standard Bethe-Heitler scattering process.

**Main result.** The authors successfully identified hundreds of GHZ and W states in the process, demonstrating that the BH process serves as a viable, albeit highly constrained, laboratory for generating and studying genuine tripartite entanglement.

**Method.** The analysis involves calculating helicity amplitudes for the three-particle final state and using entanglement measures (concurrence, $F_{GHZ}^3$, $F_W^3$) to map out the phase space regions where these entangled states are formed.

**Model / system.** The system is the Bethe-Heitler process ($e+p	o e+p+\gamma$), where the final electron, proton, and photon are treated as three qubits based on their helicity states. Entanglement is built up through successive elementary interactions.

**Key observables.** Bipartite concurrences ($C_{\ell p}, C_{\ell \gamma}, C_{p\gamma}$), genuine tripartite entanglement measures ($F_{GHZ}^3, F_W^3$), and the local-unitary fidelity ($F$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Center-of-mass energy ($\sqrt{s} < 5$ GeV), mixing angles ($\alpha_e, \alpha_p$), and specific kinematic regimes (e.g., backward scattering, collinear emission).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on identifying local minima in the high-dimensional phase space and estimating the volume and overlap rates of these discrete entangled regions.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the distribution of found GHZ and W states in various parameter planes (e.g., $(\alpha_e, \alpha_p)$ plane) and compare the phase space volumes of entangled states versus bipartite states.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically analyzes the entanglement generation via two-step processes, calculates entanglement measures, identifies specific kinematic conditions for maximal entanglement, and finally quantifies the phase space volume occupied by the discrete GHZ and W states compared to continuous bipartite entanglement regions.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

The familiar Bethe-Heitler process on the proton target $e+p\to e+p+γ$ is transformed into a laboratory for studying multiparticle entanglement. We discuss how bipartite and genuine tripartite entanglement between the final state electron, proton and photon are built up by successive $1\to 2$ and $2\to 2$ elementary interactions. We validate our argument by simulating events. Below 5 GeV center-of-mass energy, we identify more than 900 Greenberger-Horne-Zeilinger (GHZ) states and 1200 W states, each with a fidelty exceeding 99%.

</details>

**Authors:** Fuyuki Kitagawa, Ryo Nishimaki, Agi Villanyi, Takashi Yamakawa  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** other · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2608.17610v1>

**Summary.** This paper proves the impossibility of certain quantum key agreement schemes in the QROM by constructing unconditional quantum attacks. Using advanced techniques like heavy-query learning, the authors show that if the agreement probability is not perfect, the key can be recovered by an unbounded attacker. This result has direct implications for the security of quantum public-key encryption.

**Why it may be interesting.** While highly theoretical, the rigorous analysis of information-theoretic security boundaries in quantum cryptography provides foundational tools for understanding the limits of quantum communication protocols.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates the theoretical impossibility of achieving imperfectly complete Quantum-Computation, Classical-Communication (QCCC) key agreement protocols within the Quantum Random Oracle Model (QROM).

**Main result.** It rules out imperfectly correct quantum public-key encryption (QPKE) for classical messages whose length is polynomially bounded in the QROM under specific conditions.

**Method.** The analysis constructs unconditional quantum attacks by combining heavy-query learning techniques with random reprogramming lemmas.

**Model / system.** The model is the QROM applied to key agreement protocols, considering settings like two-message and multi-round interactions where parties perform quantum computations and make oracle queries.

**Key observables.** The valid agreement probability ($\alpha_{\Pi}(\lambda)$) and the attacker's ability to recover the shared key.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The query bounds ($q_{A,1}, q_{B}, q_{A,2}$, etc.) and the inverse-polynomial nature of the valid agreement probability.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The attacker is computationally unbounded, and the honest query bounds are polynomially bounded in $\lambda$.

**Figures summary.** Not specified, but the notes detail theorems and corollaries establishing quantitative bounds on the advantage of the attack.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by analyzing specific settings (two-message, multi-round) to establish attack bounds, culminating in the impossibility result for QPKE.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We make progress towards the impossibility of imperfectly complete quantum-computation, classical-communication (QCCC) key agreement by constructing the first unconditional attacks on quantum key agreement in the following restricted settings. In the two-message setting, we assume that Alice makes only classical queries to the oracle in the first round and that her message to Bob is classical, but otherwise both parties may perform arbitrary quantum computation, make quantum queries, and send a quantum state in the second round. Our attack and analysis are based on the heavy-query learning techniques from Austrin et al. (CRYPTO 2022) and the reprogramming techniques of Katz and Sela (arXiv 2401.14319). In the round-independent setting, we show that the attack of Barak and Mahmoody (CRYPTO 2009; J. Cryptology 2017) can be extended to multiple rounds when Alice and Bob share classical communication and make only classical queries in all but the final round. In both settings, the attacker is computationally unbounded and makes $poly(λ)$ queries to recover the key whenever each honest query bound is at most $poly(λ)$ and the valid agreement probability is inverse-polynomial. As a consequence, we rule out imperfectly correct quantum public-key encryption for classical messages whose length is bounded by a polynomial in $λ$ in the QROM when key generation has classical oracle access, even if encryption, decryption, and the ciphertext are quantum. In particular, the one-bit case applies to the imperfectly correct PKE obtained from two-round OSP by Bartusek and Khurana (CRYPTO 2025) whenever the classical OSP sender makes only classical random-oracle queries.

</details>

**Summary.** This research analyzes decades of solar wind proton density data to characterize its statistical and dynamical properties using nonextensive statistical mechanics. By calculating the q-triplet, the authors confirm persistent heavy-tailed fluctuations and slow relaxation. Crucially, they demonstrate that these plasma properties are modulated by the solar cycle, showing a strong periodicity linked to solar activity.

**Why it may be interesting.** The application of nonextensive statistics and multifractal analysis to space plasma dynamics provides a rigorous, non-equilibrium framework for characterizing turbulence and long-range correlations in astrophysical plasmas.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To investigate the long-term evolution and solar cycle dependence of nonlinear statistical and dynamical properties of solar wind plasma.

**Main result.** The statistical properties, characterized by the q-triplet, confirm persistent heavy-tailed statistics and slow relaxation, showing modulation by solar activity with a dominant periodicity of $\sim 10.1$ years.

**Method.** The study uses nonextensive statistical mechanics to estimate the q-triplet ($	ext{qstat}$, $	ext{qrel}$, $	ext{qsens}$) from hourly proton density measurements using sliding time windows.

**Model / system.** The physical system is the turbulent, weakly collisional solar wind plasma, analyzed using the Wind spacecraft's proton density measurements over three decades (1995–2025). The analysis framework is nonextensive statistical mechanics.

**Key observables.** The q-triplet ($	ext{qstat}$, $	ext{qrel}$, $	ext{qsens}$), proton density increments, autocorrelation function decay, and multifractal spectrum.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Mean values: $	ext{qstat}=1.71\pm0.07$, $	ext{qrel}=4.62\pm0.52$, $	ext{qsens}=-0.45\pm0.27$. Key regimes include $q>1$ for heavy tails and slow relaxation.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on a single-spacecraft dataset (Wind) to provide an independent test, though potential orbital transition effects must be considered.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the temporal evolution of the three q-parameters, the correlation between $	ext{qstat}$ and sunspot number, and the Fourier power spectrum of $	ext{qstat}$ showing a dominant $\sim 10.1$-year peak.

**Paper structure.** The paper analyzes the time series of the q-triplet over 1995–2025, calculates correlations with solar/geomagnetic indices, and uses Fourier analysis to determine periodicity and modulation by solar activity.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

The solar wind is a turbulent, weakly collisional plasma characterized by non-Gaussian fluctuations, long-range correlations, and multifractal dynamics. We investigate the long-term evolution of these properties using hourly proton density measurements obtained directly by the Wind spacecraft over 1995--2025. The use of a single-spacecraft dataset provides an independent test of previous results derived from the multi-spacecraft OMNI database. The three components of the nonextensive $q$-triplet are estimated within one-year sliding windows shifted monthly: $q_{stat}$ from the distribution of density increments, $q_{rel}$ from the decay of the autocorrelation function, and $q_{sens}$ from the multifractal spectrum. Their mean values, $q_{stat}=1.71\pm0.07$, $q_{rel}=4.62\pm0.52$, and $q_{sens}=-0.45\pm0.27$, confirm the persistent presence of heavy-tailed statistics, slow relaxation, and weakly chaotic multifractal dynamics. The parameters nevertheless exhibit distinct temporal variability and relationships with solar activity. The Fourier spectrum of $q_{stat}$ contains a dominant period of approximately $10.1$ years, while its correlation with the sunspot number is positive and moderate ($r=0.611$). Correlation and mutual-information analyses show that $q_{stat}$ is primarily associated with solar proxies, whereas $q_{sens}$ displays stronger relationships with geomagnetic indices and $q_{rel}$ exhibits weaker dependencies. An anomalous enhancement of $q_{stat}$ around 2004 coincides with a sequence of intense interplanetary disturbances, although possible effects related to Wind's orbital transition must also be considered. These findings demonstrate the robustness of the nonextensive description and show that the statistical and dynamical properties of solar wind proton density show evidence of modulation by solar activity.

</details>

### [Unclonable encryption from BB84 states: a simultaneous Goldreich-Levin reduction](http://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17629v1)

**Authors:** Andrea Coladangelo, Qipeng Liu, Ziyi Xie  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** quantum information and computing · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2608.17629v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks

<details open><summary>📷 Fig 1</summary>

<img src="2608.17629_figures/2608.17629_fig1.jpg" width="500"><br>
<sub>Figure 1: The search game G (left) and its common-mask Goldreich–Levin variant GL(G) (right).</sub>

</details>

**Summary.** This paper develops a novel 'simultaneous Goldreich-Levin reduction' applicable to quantum cryptography when parties share a common random mask. It successfully upgrades the security guarantee of unclonable encryption schemes from a search-based security notion to the stronger indistinguishability notion. This advances the theoretical understanding of quantum cryptographic security proofs.

**Why it may be interesting.** The work provides a rigorous, information-theoretic bridge connecting two fundamental security notions (search vs. decision) in quantum cryptography, which is highly relevant for quantum information security protocols.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper aims to establish a 'simultaneous Goldreich-Levin reduction' to upgrade unclonable encryption schemes from 'search' security to the stronger 'unclonable indistinguishability' (decision) security.

**Main result.** The authors provide a general, information-theoretic reduction showing that the search security implies indistinguishability security in the common-mask setting, and demonstrate this for the simplest BB84 candidate scheme.

**Method.** The core method involves adapting the classical Goldreich-Levin reduction framework to the quantum setting, specifically addressing the challenge of a common random mask shared by two entangled parties.

**Model / system.** The security analysis is built around unclonable encryption schemes, using the BB84 monogamy-of-entanglement game as a source of initial 'search' security.

**Key observables.** Success probabilities in the search game ($p_{	ext{Search}}$) and the common-mask GL game ($p_{	ext{Pred}}$); the relationship $\Delta = 2p_{	ext{Pred}} - 1$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The reduction yields bounds like $p_{	ext{Search}} \geq \Delta^4$, and later theorems introduce parameters like $\gamma$ and $L_{\max}$ for efficiency analysis.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes the existence of a common-mask GL reduction and later theorems rely on assumptions regarding the computability of parameters ($\gamma$) or the ability to truncate sampling ($L_{\max}$).

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the conceptual difference between the search game G and its common-mask GL variant GL(G).

**Paper structure.** The paper develops the general reduction from the GL game to the search game, proving the existence of the reduction ($p_{	ext{Search}} \geq \Delta^4$). Subsequent sections refine this reduction, providing uniform and truncated variants that improve efficiency and robustness.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Goldreich-Levin reductions are ubiquitous in cryptography: they convert an algorithm capable of guessing $\langle r, m \rangle$ (mod $2$) for a hidden string $m$ and a random challenge $r$, to one that is capable of extracting the entirety of $m$. Here, we describe a "simultaneous" Goldreich-Levin reduction for two entangled parties who are capable of guessing $\langle r, m \rangle$ given uniformly random identical challenges $r$. This allows to upgrade any unclonable encryption scheme satisfying "search" security to one satisfying the gold standard of unclonable "indistinguishability". As a corollary, we show that the simplest candidate unclonable encryption scheme from BB84 states satisfies unclonable indistinguishability.   This result was discovered by GPT-5.6 Ultra after a few interactions. Our prompts included recent results on unclonable encryption by Ananth and Sahai, and Ragavan.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper investigates topological superconductivity in a TI nano-SQUID by measuring both global critical currents and local tunneling conductance. While global measurements show clear, flux-tunable signatures consistent with a topological phase transition, the local measurements yield ambiguous results. The authors conclude that a combined, integrated approach is necessary to definitively confirm the presence of Majorana bound states.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses the experimental challenge of verifying topological superconductivity by comparing macroscopic (global) transport signatures with microscopic (local) spectroscopic signatures, which is highly relevant to open quantum systems research.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study aims to investigate and conclusively identify topological superconductivity, motivated by the search for Majorana bound states (MBSs), in a solid-state platform.

**Main result.** Global superconducting signatures (e.g., $h/2e$ flux periodicity and SDE reversal) are observed, suggesting a topological phase transition, but local measurements show ambiguous zero-bias peaks that do not correlate robustly with global signatures.

**Method.** The research combines global transport measurements (critical current, superconducting diode effect) with local tunneling spectroscopy and nonlocal conductance measurements on a nano-SQUID structure.

**Model / system.** The physical system is a three-dimensional topological insulator (TI) nanowire proximitized by a superconductor, configured as a nano-SQUID. The system's superconducting properties are tuned by an external axial magnetic flux ($\Phi$) and gate voltage ($V_b$).

**Key observables.** Critical current ($I_c$), superconducting diode effect (SDE) polarity, zero-bias conductance peaks (ZBPs), and nonlocal conductance ($G_{LL}, G_{RL}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Axial magnetic flux ($\Phi$), gate voltage ($V_G$), and the superconducting flux quantum ($\Phi_0 = h/2e$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The authors caution that the lack of correlation between local ZBP signatures and global signatures implies that conclusively identifying MBSs requires integrating global topological property establishment with advanced local probes.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the device structure, $I_c$ vs. $B_x$ showing oscillations, and simultaneous measurements comparing global $I_c$ anomalies with local conductance spectral jumps and nonlocal conductance changes across different flux regimes.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically presents global measurements demonstrating flux-tunable topological signatures, followed by local spectroscopy confirming spectral jumps, and concludes by analyzing the correlation (or lack thereof) between these local and global findings to address the ambiguity of MBS identification.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Topological systems are defined by global properties that enforce the existence of local boundary modes. Three-dimensional topological insulators (TIs) were among the earliest proposed systems for hosting topological superconductivity, but experimental focus subsequently shifted to other platforms. Here, we revisit bulk-insulating TIs using a columnar nano-superconducting quantum interference device (nano-SQUID) architecture. This geometry optimises the proximity effect on the TI surface and enables simultaneous probing of global superconducting properties - via the critical current through the nano-SQUID - alongside the local states at the ends of the nano-SQUID via tunnel junctions. We observe several global superconducting features that appear to show a flux-driven global phase transition consistent with entering the topological regime, including periodic critical current oscillations and a sign reversal in the superconducting diode effect. Simultaneously, tunnelling spectroscopy reveals spectral jumps in local and nonlocal conductance that align with these global features. However, zero-bias peaks (ZBPs) in local conductance are present both within the predicted topological range of magnetic fields and in theoretically trivial regimes, including at zero magnetic field. Ultimately, the lack of correlation between local ZBP signatures and global signatures emphasises that conclusively identifying Majorana bound states will necessitate a combined approach, integrating the establishment of global topological properties with the use of local and other, more advanced, probes.

</details>

**Summary.** This work investigates the stabilization and control of high-frequency magnetic droplets, which are nonlinear spin-wave solitons, within nanoscale Spin Hall Nano-Oscillators. Using micromagnetic simulations, the authors show that by carefully tuning the applied magnetic field, they can robustly stabilize these droplets in a non-propagating state, and further control their transition into propagating modes. This offers a pathway for advanced spintronic devices for both local memory and interconnect applications.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on classical magnetism, the study of localized, nonlinear wave packets (solitons) and their stability under external driving forces shares mathematical parallels with nonlinear wave phenomena studied in quantum optics and open quantum systems.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary goal was to demonstrate the robust stabilization of non-propagating, high-frequency magnetic droplets in pure spin current-driven devices, specifically Spin Hall Nano-Oscillators (SHNOs), which was previously difficult to achieve.

**Main result.** The authors successfully demonstrated stable, non-propagating magnetic droplets in W/CoFeB/MgO nanoconstriction SHNOs by tuning the applied magnetic field, and showed that these droplets can transition into propagating solitons under different field conditions.

**Method.** The study relies on micromagnetic simulations solving the Landau–Lifshitz–Gilbert–Slonczewski (LLGS) equation to model the complex spin-wave dynamics.

**Model / system.** The physical system is a W/CoFeB/MgO nanoconstriction SHNO exhibiting strong perpendicular magnetic anisotropy (PMA). The dynamics are governed by the LLGS equation, and the stability is critically dependent on the nonlinearity coefficient, N.

**Key observables.** Auto-oscillation frequency, spectral sidebands, hysteresis loops, magnetization snapshots showing core reversal, and the transition between localized (non-propagating) and propagating droplet states.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Applied magnetic field orientation and strength, current density ($I_{dc}$), and the material's PMA strength.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The stability of the droplets is critically linked to achieving a large negative nonlinearity coefficient (N) within the nanoconstriction geometry.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the SHNO schematic, the calculated nonlinearity coefficient (N) dependence, current-sweep spectra showing hysteresis, and magnetization snapshots demonstrating droplet nucleation, breathing, and drift.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by establishing the theoretical framework (LLGS equation, nonlinearity N), presenting the simulation results showing stable droplet regimes (Region II), and finally detailing the control mechanisms by tuning the external magnetic field to induce propagation (Region III).

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Magnetic droplets are highly nonlinear spin-wave solitons that can be excited in nanoscale spintronic devices with strong perpendicular magnetic anisotropy. Although extensively studied in nanocontact-based spin-torque oscillators, their stabilization in pure spin current-driven devices such as spin Hall nano-oscillators (SHNOs) has remained elusive. Here, we micromagnetically demonstrate the robust stabilization of non-propagating high-frequency droplets in CMOS-compatible W/CoFeB/MgO nanoconstriction SHNOs under oblique magnetic fields. While the constriction geometry gives rise to noncircular droplet shapes in an inhomogeneous effective field landscape, stable droplet modes exhibiting complete core magnetization reversal and pronounced hysteresis are observed. At lower current densities, droplets display breathing oscillations with periodic expansion and contraction, whereas higher drive currents lead to drift, deformation, and the emergence of sidebands around the fundamental frequency. Tuning the strength and orientation of the applied magnetic field alters the effective field landscape, allowing droplets to escape confinement and propagate over distances exceeding 2$μ$m.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper reports on the design and experimental realization of a solid-state refrigerator that cools silicon chips to sub-Kelvin temperatures using arrays of NIS superconducting junctions. The methodology involves modeling quasi-particle diffusion and meticulously engineering the interfaces to minimize parasitic heat leakage. The success demonstrates a significant advance in solid-state cryocooling technology.

**Why it may be interesting.** The detailed modeling of non-equilibrium heat transport, involving energy filtering via superconducting gaps and diffusive quasi-particle dynamics, touches upon non-equilibrium statistical mechanics and energy relaxation processes relevant to quantum thermodynamics.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To develop a scalable, solid-state refrigerator capable of cooling silicon chips to temperatures below 1 K by efficiently managing heat transfer and minimizing parasitic heat leakage.

**Main result.** The authors demonstrated cooling of a silicon chip down to 70 mK and achieved a cooling of -97 mK, marking the first demonstration of sub-1 K cooling of an entire silicon chip using NIS junctions.

**Method.** The work combines experimental fabrication of complex superconducting junctions with advanced 3D numerical simulations to model quasi-particle diffusion and interfacial heat flow.

**Model / system.** The system is a chip-scale refrigerator built from Normal-metal/Insulator/Superconductor (NIS) junctions, configured as SINIS units, which operate by filtering electrons via the superconducting energy gap. Heat transport is modeled using diffusive quasi-particle flow, incorporating both Acoustic Mismatch Models (AMM) and specific interfacial heat flow equations.

**Key observables.** Effective phonon temperature of the silicon chip, Coefficient of Performance (COP), and 2nd Law Efficiency.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Operating temperatures down to 70 mK, junction resistance ($	ext{621 } \Omega\cdot\mu	ext{m}^2$), and the superconducting energy gap ($\Delta$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The primary assumptions involve modeling quasi-particle flow as diffusive and engineering the geometry to limit heat leakage, though some models ignore quasiparticle branching mixing effects.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the lithography masks and cross-sections of the cooler, schematics of the SINIS unit, and comparative plots showing simulated vs. experimental phonon temperature profiles validating the heat leakage model.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from establishing the physical limitations of existing coolers to detailing the NIS junction physics, developing sophisticated models for heat leakage, and culminating in the measurement and validation of sub-Kelvin cooling performance metrics.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Solid-state refrigerators using Normal-metal/Insulator/Superconductor (NIS) junctions have previously demonstrated excellent electron cooling but limited ability to cool phonons. The energy gap of the superconductor is used as an energy filter to allow higher than average energy electrons to preferentially tunnel from the normal-metal through the insulator into the superconductor where they travel as quasi-particles. Typically, the heat is moved and work is done to deposit hot quasi-particles into a normal-metal quasi-particle trap for rejection to the next refrigeration stage. Realizing that (1) the quasi-particles flow diffusively, driven by a concentration gradient in the electric field-free superconductor, and (2) that the undesirable backwards leaking of heat from the hot-side trap can be reduced by engineering the geometry and materials at the superconductor-to-trap interface, enhanced cooling can be achieved. Fabrication of the refrigerator was accomplished using a tungsten and titanium-tungsten alloy as the cold-side normal-metal, aluminum oxide as the insulator, aluminum as the superconductor, and gold as the trap, with the cold-side NIS portion being attached to the hot-side gold trap by bump bonding. The refrigerator consisted of 1121 junction pairs, each pair being an SINIS unit, all electrically connected in series. Using this we have measured the effective phonon temperature of a 3.9 mm x 3.9 mm x 0.65 mm silicon chip driven down to 70 mK from a bath temperature of 120 mK, and down to 174 mK from a 271 mK rejection temperature (a cooling of -97 mK). This is the first demonstration of the sub 1 K cooling of an entire silicon chip using NIS junctions.

</details>

**Summary.** This work demonstrates that twisted bilayer graphene exhibits rich electronic physics near the crossover between quasicrystalline and moiré order at $	heta \approx 29^\circ$. By combining TEM and magnetotransport, the authors show that quasiperiodicity fundamentally alters low-energy states, evidenced by an anomalous, temperature-dependent evolution of the Landau-level degeneracy. This challenges existing models by proving that strong interlayer coupling persists even outside the conventional moiré regime.

**Why it may be interesting.** The observation of temperature-dependent, high-fold Landau level degeneracy in a quasiperiodic system provides a rich platform for studying emergent many-body physics and how geometric symmetry dictates correlated electronic behavior, relevant to understanding topological phases.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates the electronic reconstruction in twisted bilayer graphene (TBG) at a twist angle ($\approx 29^\circ$) that lies between the quasicrystalline and commensurate moiré regimes.

**Main result.** The conventional view that large-angle TBG is electronically trivial breaks down, as the system exhibits strong, temperature-dependent interlayer hybridization leading to an anomalous evolution of Landau-level degeneracy from fourfold to twelvefold.

**Method.** The study combines atomic-resolution Transmission Electron Microscopy (TEM) to map structural symmetry with magnetotransport measurements (Shubnikov-de Haas oscillations) to probe electronic states.

**Model / system.** The physical system is TBG, focusing on the crossover regime near $29^\circ$. The physics involves Umklapp scattering mediating interlayer coupling, which fundamentally reshapes the low-energy electronic states beyond standard moiré band theory.

**Key observables.** Landau-level degeneracy (evolving from 4-fold to 12-fold), magneto-intersubband oscillations (MISO), and the deviation from expected transport signatures at $29^\circ$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Twist angle ($	heta$), specifically the crossover point near $29^\circ$, and temperature ($T$) dependence.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes that the observed transport anomalies are due to the interplay between quasiperiodic symmetry and interlayer Umklapp scattering, challenging models based solely on decoupled or purely commensurate structures.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the structural transition from 12-fold quasicrystal symmetry ($30^\circ$) through the $29^\circ$ crossover state to the commensurate moiré lattice ($27.8^\circ$), alongside transport maps showing the anomalous evolution of quantum Hall plateaus.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the structural context via TEM, then uses magnetotransport measurements to probe the electronic consequences of the quasicrystal-moiré crossover, culminating in the observation of temperature-dependent Landau level degeneracy.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Large twist angles in twisted bilayer graphene are widely expected to be electronically trivial, with negligible interlayer coupling and no electronic reconstruction, in contrast to the rich moiré-driven band reconstruction and correlated physics that emerge at small twist angles. Here, we show that this paradigm breaks down near a twist angle of 29°, where the system crosses over between quasicrystalline and commensurate order. Atomic-resolution transmission electron microscopy directly reveals the coexistence of near-dodecagonal quasicrystalline symmetry and emerging moiré periodicity, indicating an intermediate, nonperiodic structural regime. Magnetotransport measurements uncover strong interlayer hybridization mediated by Umklapp scattering, manifested by magneto-intersubband oscillations and a highly unconventional Landau-level spectrum. Remarkably, the Landau-level degeneracy evolves from 4- to 12-fold with increasing temperature, a behavior incompatible with two decoupled graphene monolayers. These findings establish large-angle twisted bilayer graphene as a platform where quasiperiodic symmetry fundamentally reshapes low-energy electronic states beyond the conventional moiré framework.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper introduces the Deformed Image Vortex Ansatz (DIVA) to improve the theoretical description of magnetic vortices in nanodots under external fields. By building the field response directly into the magnetization profile, DIVA provides a significantly more accurate model than previous methods. The work validates this improved theory by comparing its predictions against detailed micromagnetic simulations.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on classical magnetism, the methodology of building the perturbation response directly into the ansatz, rather than adding it as a correction, mirrors advanced techniques used in developing effective Hamiltonians or variational wavefunctions in quantum many-body physics.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Existing magnetization ansätze fail to accurately describe how magnetic vortices deform when subjected to external in-plane magnetic fields.

**Main result.** The proposed Deformed Image Vortex Ansatz (DIVA) significantly reduces the error in describing vortex deformation compared to previous ansätze, showing improvements by factors of 3 to 6 in angular deviation.

**Method.** The authors developed a perturbation-aware ansatz (DIVA) and minimized the total energy functional by solving a resulting differential equation for the radial profile of the deformation.

**Model / system.** The study models magnetic vortices in thin ferromagnetic nanodots (Permalloy) subjected to an external in-plane field. The physics is governed by minimizing the total energy functional, which includes exchange, Zeeman, and magnetostatic terms.

**Key observables.** Disk-averaged angular deviation ($\delta\Phi$), total-energy deviation, and the radial profile function $f(r)$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Aspect ratio ($t/R$), external field strength ($H$), and material parameters like saturation magnetization ($M_s$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The deformation is assumed to be dominated by the $m=1$ harmonic, and the magnetostatic energy is approximated using a local functional form.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 schematically compares the local magnetization directions for the two-vortex ansatz and DIVA under an in-plane field. Other figures show energy decompositions and the dependence of the demagnetizing factor on aspect ratio.

**Paper structure.** The paper first identifies the limitation of existing ansätze, introduces the DIVA, derives the governing radial equation by energy minimization, and finally benchmarks the solution against micromagnetic simulations to validate the improved accuracy.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Thiele-based descriptions of magnetic vortex dynamics in thin ferromagnetic nanodots rely on magnetization ansätze that describe the equilibrium texture but cannot represent perturbation-induced deformations. We introduce the Deformed Image Vortex Ansatz (DIVA): a perturbation-aware ansatz in which the response to an external perturbation is built into the magnetization profile itself, rather than appended to the dynamics as a correction. Here, we demonstrate the concept for a uniform, stationary in-plane field applied to a Permalloy nanodot, for which the deformation is analytically tractable. A symmetry-based perturbative expansion identifies the leading deformation as a single $m = 1$ harmonic around the disk, while energy minimization and a dominant-balance analysis yield a closed-form interpolant for the radial profile. Benchmarked against micromagnetic simulations on Permalloy disks of aspect ratio $t/R = 0.1$ and $0.0125$, this realization reduces the disk-averaged angular deviation by a factor of 3 to 6 relative to the two-vortex ansatz, depending on geometry and field, and reduces the total-energy deviation by about a factor of six in the thicker disk.

</details>

**Summary.** This work proposes that the thermal emissivity of metallic nanowire networks is not governed by simple DC resistance but by a geometric inductance ($X_L$) derived from the average inter-wire gap ($g$). By modeling the network as an inductive mesh, the authors successfully unify disparate experimental results onto a single universal curve, establishing the gap size as the master design variable for radiative properties.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on classical electromagnetism and condensed matter optics, the concept of a single, non-material-constant geometric parameter ($g$) controlling a complex macroscopic property (emissivity) is analogous to finding an effective Hamiltonian or controlling parameter in open quantum systems.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper aims to explain the previously unexplained regularities in the thermal infrared emissivity of metallic nanowire networks, which are candidates for low-emissivity transparent electrodes.

**Main result.** The thermal emissivity is shown to be governed by a geometric inductance ($X_L$) derived from the average inter-wire gap ($g$), collapsing multiple experimental datasets onto a single universal curve.

**Method.** The authors developed a model based on transmission-line theory and magnetostatics, treating the network as an inductive mesh structure rather than relying solely on DC electrical transport properties.

**Model / system.** The system is metallic nanowire networks (e.g., AgNWs) forming a mesh. The key theoretical element is the geometric inductance $X_L = Z_0 (g/\lambda) \ln(2g/\pi D)$, which acts as the master variable controlling the optical response.

**Key observables.** Thermal infrared emissivity ($\epsilon$), optical impedance ($Z_{opt}$), and sheet resistance ($R_s$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Average inter-wire gap ($g$), thermal wavelength ($\lambda$), and wire diameter ($D$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The optical response is governed by the local geometric inductance ($X_L$), which is assumed to be the dominant factor, decoupling the optical behavior from the global electrical percolation threshold.

**Figures summary.** Figures demonstrate the universal collapse of emissivity vs. $X_L/Z_0$, show the divergence between $R_s$ and $|Z_{opt}|$ at high density, and illustrate the length-independence of the optical response.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the puzzle (anomalous $\epsilon$ dependence), develops the theory based on geometric inductance ($X_L$), validates $X_L$ by reproducing multiple datasets, and contrasts this finding with failing models like the Hanauer model.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

The thermal infrared emissivity of metallic nanowire networks - the property that makes them candidate low-emissivity transparent electrodes - follows regularities that have resisted physical explanation: a strong wire-diameter dependence with no wire-length dependence, a correlation with sheet resistance that breaks down above ~15 ohm/sq, and an angular signature that turns from dielectric-like to metallic as the network densifies. We show that all of them follow from one local geometric length, the average inter-wire gap g, acting through the geometric inductance of a mesh finer than the thermal wavelength: $X_L = Z_0 (g/λ) \ln(2g/πD)$, a parameter-free reactance that exceeds the ohmic loss and, unlike the DC sheet resistance, stays finite instead of diverging at the percolation threshold. Since the gap is fixed by areal density and wire diameter alone, the optics is length-independent and decoupled from percolative transport; what has been read as a second, optical percolation is instead a smooth impedance crossing. With a single fitted parameter the theory reproduces 56 silver-nanowire samples across four diameters to a mean absolute error of 0.04, collapses them onto one universal curve, and - unchanged - predicts the emissivity-transmittance data of five independent groups and the measured spectral emissivity, including the silica phonon band. Because this reactance contains no material constant, the inter-wire gap emerges as the master design variable for the radiative properties of metallic nanowire transparent conductors.

</details>
