**Summary.** This paper analyzes the variable-mass sine-Gordon model incorporating point defects to study its conservation laws. Using advanced techniques from integrable systems, the authors show that exact integrability is lost unless specific parameters match, leading to a regime of charge-dependent quasi-conservation. This provides a theoretical framework for understanding soliton transport in inhomogeneous media.

**Why it may be interesting.** The framework establishes a detailed mechanism for how exact integrability breaks down into controlled quasi-conservation due to defects, which has direct implications for modeling physical systems like Josephson junctions or nonlinear optics.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates the integrability, soliton transmission, and quasi-conservation properties of the variable-mass sine-Gordon model when point defects are introduced.

**Main result.** Exact defect integrability is maintained only when a specific closure condition ($\alphaeta = 1$) is met; otherwise, the system exhibits charge-dependent quasi-conservation.

**Method.** The authors employed Lax and Bäcklund-gauge formulations to derive conserved charges, followed by numerical simulations on a two-half-line geometry to analyze defect-induced anomalies.

**Model / system.** The model is the variable-mass sine-Gordon equation with point defects, which is analyzed using techniques from integrable systems theory, including defect matrices and sewing conditions.

**Key observables.** Conserved charges (Energy, Momentum), the soliton transmission factor ($z$), and defect-localized anomalies.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Deformation parameters $\alpha$ and $eta$, which control the transition between integrable and non-integrable defect regimes.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes the existence of a Lax pair and that outer boundaries are far enough from the defect such that generated signals do not return during the simulation time.

**Figures summary.** The notes reference figures showing finite-difference results, illustrating the transmission/conversion regimes and the effects of non-integrable defects.

**Paper structure.** The work progresses from establishing the infinite hierarchy of conserved charges in the standard sine-Gordon model using Lax pairs, to generalizing this to the variable-mass case, and finally introducing deformations ($\alpha, eta$) and numerical simulations to study the transition to quasi-conservation at defects.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We study integrable variable-mass sine-Gordon model (vmSG) with point defects. Using the Lax and Bäcklund-gauge formulations, we construct type-I and type-II defect matrices, derive the corresponding sewing conditions, and generate the bulk and defect contributions to an infinite hierarchy of conserved charges. The lowest members reduce to the standard sine-Gordon energy and momentum in the homogeneous limit, while for inhomogeneous backgrounds they define integrability-generated energy- and momentum-type quantities. Defect compatibility imposes matching conditions on the variable-mass functions across the defect. An analytical transmission factor $z$ characterizes soliton transmission, topological conversion, and absorption/emission processes. We then deform the type-I sewing conditions by parameters $α$ and $β$ and derive the associated defect anomalies. Consistent one-soliton transmission is recovered for $αβ=1$, whereas for $αβ\neq 1$ parity-centered kink-kink and kink-antikink transmissions display vanishing lowest order integrated anomalies but no generic vanishing at higher order. Numerical simulations reproduce the integrable transmission/conversion regimes and show that non-integrable defects generate weak radiative tails and lowest order quasi-conserved charges, while quasi-conservation of the higher order charges remains unestablished. These results elucidate how exact defect integrability deforms into charge-dependent quasi-conservation and establish a framework for defect-controlled soliton transport in inhomogeneous media, with potential applications to nonuniform Josephson junctions, magnetic and nonlinear-optical systems, and effective molecular and DNA models.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper analyzes vector Akhmediev breathers in the coupled Sasa-Satsuma system, a model for coupled optical pulses. By comparing the spectral analysis of breathers with the linear modulation instability spectrum, the authors establish a deep link: the condition for zero linear gain precisely corresponds to the state-transition condition for the breathers. This unifies the understanding of stable and unstable wave states in this nonlinear system.

**Why it may be interesting.** The connection drawn between the state-transition condition (from breathers) and the zero-gain condition (from MI) provides a powerful unified diagnostic tool for understanding nonlinear wave dynamics in optical systems.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates the existence and properties of vector Akhmediev breathers (ABs) and the associated state transitions within the coupled Sasa-Satsuma (CSS) system.

**Main result.** The analysis establishes a direct correspondence between admissible characteristic-root pairs derived from the spectral analysis and the linear Modulation Instability (MI) branches, showing that zero-gain MI corresponds to periodic-wave states.

**Method.** The study employs spectral analysis using Lax pairs, Darboux transformations to construct breathers, and linear stability analysis to determine the MI gain spectrum.

**Model / system.** The governing equation is the coupled Sasa-Satsuma (CSS) system, which models the coupled propagation of two ultrashort optical pulse envelopes. The analysis is performed across three distinct carrier-wavenumber configurations.

**Key observables.** Vector Akhmediev Breathers (ABs), MI gain $G(\chi_a; \gamma)$, and the localization variable $\Psi$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Carrier wavenumbers ($eta_1, eta_2$), spectral parameters ($\lambda$), and the degeneracy/nondegeneracy of the background state.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes specific configurations for the background carrier wavenumbers and relies on the equivalence between MI conditions and characteristic-root equations.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the existence diagram in the $(eta_2, \gamma)$ plane for the case $eta_1 = 0$, distinguishing between degenerate and nondegenerate regimes.

**Paper structure.** The paper follows a structure moving from spectral analysis (defining the background), to classifying AB branches based on wavenumber configurations, then analyzing the linear modulation instability (MI) spectrum, and finally establishing the correspondence between the two.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We investigate vector Akhmediev breathers (ABs) in the degenerate and nondegenerate regimes for the coupled Sasa-Satsuma system describing two coupled ultrashort optical pulse envelopes. Based on the carrier-wavenumber relations of the two-component background, we examine three configurations: a single vanishing wavenumber, a pair of opposite wavenumbers, and two nonzero wavenumbers of unequal magnitudes. These configurations admit distinct maximal degrees of nondegeneracy. For each configuration, we derive the reduced spectral equations, construct the existence diagrams, and compare the resulting branches with the modulation-instability gain spectra. Owing to the distinct carrier-wavenumber symmetries, nondegenerate-type modes can occur in spectrally degenerate regions, whereas degenerate-type ones may also appear in spectrally nondegenerate regions. Moreover, all admissible branches in the degenerate regimes satisfy the state-transition condition and correspond to periodic-wave states, while such branches are also present in nondegenerate regimes. Direct numerical simulations of representative solutions further confirm the analytical predictions.

</details>

**Summary.** This study reveals that the bandgap of a flat-band solid can undergo giant, periodic pulsations at room temperature due to lattice vibrations. The effect is governed by a dual selection mechanism: the strong coupling of the $	ext{A}_{1g}$ breathing phonon and the phase coherence of the $\Gamma$-point lattice motion. This demonstrates that ordered electronic responses can emerge from seemingly disordered thermal fluctuations.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work connects lattice dynamics (phonons) directly to electronic structure modulation on femtosecond timescales, providing a mechanism for intrinsic, thermally driven ultrafast electronic functionality, which is relevant to understanding energy conversion in complex materials.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates whether intrinsic, thermal lattice vibrations alone can induce a pronounced, periodic, and large-amplitude pulsation in the electronic bandgap of a solid, challenging the view of the bandgap as a static property.

**Main result.** A giant bandgap pulsation ($\sim 0.95$ eV) was observed at room temperature, driven by a dual selection mechanism: the strong coupling of the $	ext{A}_{1g}$-like breathing phonon mode and the dominance of the unit-cell-synchronous $\Gamma$-point component over finite-$q$ modes.

**Method.** The research employs *ab initio* molecular dynamics (AIMD) simulations combined with first-principles electron-phonon calculations to track the time evolution of the Conduction Band Minimum (CBM) energy.

**Model / system.** The system studied is a perovskite-like flat-band solid, specifically the DMSO crystal, which contains $	ext{[TeCl}_6]^{2-}$ octahedra responsible for the flat conduction band minimum.

**Key observables.** Electronic bandgap dynamics (pulsation amplitude and frequency), CBM energy modulation $\delta E(t)$, and the correlation between CBM energy and the $	ext{A}_{1g}$ phonon coordinate.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Room temperature (300 K), pulsation frequency ($\sim 7.8$ THz), and maximum peak-to-peak variation ($\sim 0.95$ eV).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes that the observed dynamics are governed by the coupling between the electronic structure and the lattice vibrations, and that the $\Gamma$-point component dominates the macroscopic response.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the temporal evolution of the CBM oscillation, comparing it to the $	ext{A}_{1g}$ phonon mode coefficient, and demonstrating the phase-space difference between $\Gamma$-point and finite-$q$ mode contributions.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by establishing the problem (static vs. dynamic bandgap), presenting the simulation methodology (AIMD, e-ph coupling), identifying the key phonon mode ($	ext{A}_{1g}$), and finally proving the dual selection mechanism (strong coupling + $\Gamma$-point dominance) responsible for the large pulsation.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

The electronic bandgap of solids is conventionally viewed as a static property at a given temperature, with only weak and stochastic thermal fluctuations under equilibrium conditions. Here, using ab initio molecular dynamics and first-principles electron-phonon calculations, we reveal a pronounced room-temperature bandgap pulsation at about 7.8 THz in a perovskite-like flat-band solid, with a maximum peak-to-peak variation approaching 0.95 eV. This behavior originates from a dual selection mechanism: the A1g-like breathing branch couples much more strongly to the flat conduction-band edge than other phonon branches, while real-space phase selectivity distinguishes its hotspot gamma-point and finite-q components. Although finite-q modes retain appreciable microscopic coupling, their intercell phase shifts produce smaller-amplitude shorter-recurrence-period responses, leaving the unit-cell-synchronous gamma-point A1g component to dominate the fundamental-period bandgap pulsation. The resulting band-edge dynamics further modulates the optical response on femtosecond timescales. These findings demonstrate that an unexpectedly ordered electronic response can emerge from intrinsically disordered thermal lattice fluctuations.

</details>

**Summary.** This work uses high-pressure electronic Raman spectroscopy to track the evolution of the SDW state in $	ext{La}_3	ext{Ni}_2	ext{O}_7$. It reveals that while the SDW energy scale increases with pressure, the coherence of the SDW state simultaneously decreases. This provides key insights into the magnetic correlations relevant to the high-temperature superconductivity emerging in this nickelate system.

**Why it may be interesting.** The observation of a robust strong-coupling ratio alongside a weakening of long-range order provides crucial spectroscopic constraints on the magnetic correlations that are believed to mediate superconductivity in nickelates.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates how the Spin-Density-Wave (SDW) state in the bilayer nickelate $	ext{La}_3	ext{Ni}_2	ext{O}_7$ evolves as hydrostatic pressure is applied toward the superconducting regime.

**Main result.** The SDW gap energy and transition temperature both increase with pressure, indicating an enhanced energy scale, while the peak broadens, suggesting a weakening of long-range SDW order.

**Method.** Systematic electronic Raman spectroscopy was performed on single crystals under hydrostatic pressures up to 16.51 GPa.

**Model / system.** The physical system is the bilayer nickelate $	ext{La}_3	ext{Ni}_2	ext{O}_7$, whose low-energy physics is modeled by a bilayer $t-J$ model. The research focuses on the competition and evolution between the SDW state and superconductivity.

**Key observables.** SDW gap energy ($\Delta_{	ext{SDW}}$), SDW transition temperature ($T_{	ext{SDW}}$), dimensionless coupling ratio ($2\Delta_{	ext{SDW}}/(k_B T_{	ext{SDW}})$), and Raman peak linewidth ($\Gamma_{	ext{SDW}}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Hydrostatic pressure (up to 16.51 GPa); the strong-coupling ratio ($\sim 7.5$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes that the observed Raman response is dominated by the SDW electronic excitation continuum, which is fitted using a Lorentzian function.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 shows the pressure-temperature phase diagram, confirming $T_{	ext{SDW}}$ increases with pressure. Figure 2 details the pressure-dependent $	ext{B}_{1g}$-symmetric Raman response, showing both energy shift and broadening.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically measures the electronic Raman response across a wide pressure range, analyzing the pressure dependence of the SDW gap, transition temperature, and coherence, and interpreting these trends in the context of the SDW-superconductivity interplay.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

The discovery of pressure-induced high temperature superconductivity in the bilayer nickelate La$_{3}$Ni$_{2}$O$_{7}$ has raised the question of how its spin-density-wave (SDW) state evolves toward the superconducting regime. Here, we report a systematic electronic Raman study of La$_{3}$Ni$_{2}$O$_{7}$ single crystals under hydrostatic pressures up to 16.51 GPa. Both the SDW gap energy and the transition temperature $T_{\mathrm{SDW}}$ show an overall increase with pressure, while the dimensionless coupling ratio 2$Δ_{\text{SDW}}/(k_{\text{B}}T_{\text{SDW}})$ remains constant around $\sim7.5$, indicating a robust strong-coupling character of SDW state. At the same time, the Raman SDW peak broadens as pressure is applied, indicating a gradual weakening of long-range SDW order. These results reveal an unusual pressure evolution in which the SDW energy scale is enhanced while the SDW state becomes progressively less coherent, providing spectroscopic constraints on the magnetic correlations relevant to superconductivity in bilayer nickelates.

</details>
